" CAPITAL " CHAT.

When Mr. James Payn confessed the other day, in his "Gleams of Memory," that he earned .£2,000 a year by his literary work, it is to be regretted that he did not distinguish between his earnings by literature proper and journalism. As he is saiol to make out of one weekl journal alone, £6OO a year,a large amount of his income is not drawn direct, by virtue of his writings, from the British public ; and even if his contributions to English newspapers consisted of nothing but fiction, and therefore of pure literature in the older fashioned and narrower difinition of the term, and pecuniary results are a little misleading for those young enthusiasts who are fired by literary ambition, and who elect to win renown in their disappointment or perish an a doorstep, to cultivating. an honest calling which will leave the planet a little more habitable than it was. But, unfortunately, the would-be poet or philosopher who prefers starving to making two blades of grass grow where only one grew before, or to making a useful path for the world's commerce, is a hero, and the navvy who constructs a road for the development and civilisation of the race is des- pised and lorgotton ; and yet it is not the labour of the Keatses and the David Grays, but the labour of all the forgotten ones, which has caused the world to be the human habitation that it is.

The speculations which were so commonly in- dulged im when M. Zola's "Lourdes" was published, as to its probable reception by the Roman Catholic world, are being solved bit by bit. The Pope has relused to be interviewed by the distinguished novelist, knowing that an interview with such a person is nothing more nor less than sitting for his portrait in the future novel about Rome that M. Zola has already on the literary anvil. And now comes the announcement that the Catholics of Canada have been forbidden to read the novel. And only the other day the author and publishers were cited before the correctional tribunal by a contractor who felt himself libelled by some of the statements in the novel, and the world is awaiting the denoue- ment. As yet, however, the book has not been honoured by being put upon the " Index," and if the college does not want to stimulate its perusal the best course it can adopt is not to follow the example of the Roman Catholic authorities in Canada, but to leave the work severely alone. The kindly old father and priest who coached M. Zola in a good many of his facts about Lourdes is await- ing with some perturbation of mind the revelations which the contractor's libel suit may bring to light.

One of the most remarkable debates of the day has just taken place at Munich between a champion of orthodox Democracy, a champion of the Social Democracy, and a champion of Anarchism. The three were redoubtable men, orthodox Radicalism being defended by one of the Bavarian professors, Socialism by the famous Herr Bebel,and Anarchism by an Anarchist stonemason, who was well able to enter the lists on behalf of his opinions. The debate caused extraordinary excitement in the Bavarian capital, the audience consisting of not less than 4.000 people, and the proceeJings being reported verbatim in the Munich newspapers. All the speeches are very remarkable, there being very little fustian in any oi them, and very much close economical argument. A great boon would be con- ferred on the British public if the controversy could be presented to them in English. It shows the growth of Herr Bebel's sentiments in Germany, that, though the meeting was not a packed one, it passed a unanimous resolution in favour of his views, notwithstanding all the powerful reasoning which the exponent of orthodox Radicalism pre- sented against them. It would be interesting to have the Kaiser's thoughts on the result.

The replies which the Italian centenarians have made to a series of inquiries by a distinguished physiologist of Turin, who was anxious to arrive at the secret of their long life, will not satisfy the scientific investigator. Be addressed his questions to 382 persons who are credited with having reached the age of a hundred years, with the view of ascertaining their mode of life, the nature of their food, and their physical constitution and ap- pearance. Not more than 52 have replied. First of all, one is puzzled at finding so many centenar- ians in Italy. Most of them also are women. But seeing that on one day of the week just ended a London morning newspaper in its obituary column recorded the deaths of 18 persons of the average age of 81 years each, let it be assumed that the ages of the Italian cenr,enarians are what they say. The point in which the replies chiefly agree is that the staple food was vegetarian. But before the vege- tarians can make use of this as a conclusive argu- ment in favour of their theory, they would require to show that the masses of the community who died on this side of three score and ten were exces- sive consumers of butcher's meat. This, of course, they were not, since vegetable products are the natural food of the masses. Nor can temperance men deduce anything from another feature of the replies, which went to show that the persons ques- tioned drank only the simplest wine of the country. It may be taken for granted that they could afford no other, and the short-lived population were also condemned to similar abstemiousness.

A number of naval officials leave on Friday by the Indian mail on their way to Hong Kong. Pre- parations are being made, I am told, to have every- thing in a forward state at the dockyard there, so as to be quite prepared for any turn, Corean affairs may take. At the service clubs, the opinion is freely expressed that it will be a case of "all hands in," to use a naval phrase, before the war is over. The wish, perhaps, is father to the thought.

A large quantity of war material, including nearly a score of Gaffing guns, was shipped here a few days ago for Tamatave, in Madagascar, to the order of the Hova Government, who evidently are not quite satisfied with the attitude assumed by the French Resident General. Nearly ten years ago, after a considerable amount of fighting, the French invasion of the island was successful as far as ex- tracting a fine of £400,000 and obtaining the regula- tion of its foreign affairs were concerned. That is all. The Tamatave treaty of 1885 leaves the Hova Government quite independent in all home affairs. Whether they will brook any interference now re- mains to be seen. On the last occasion of a war with France they mustered 50,000 fighting men.

The Conservative organisers have, I hear, resolved to fall in as far as possible with Mr. Chamberlain's suggestion that they should endeavour to conciliate the Nonconformists. At this moment there are several constituencies without candidates in the so-called Unionist interest, and Mr. Chamberlain's idea is that a preference should as far as possible be given to Nonconformists. Sir G. Chubb has been persistently begged to enter the lists. There is no desire, however,te restrict the choice to Wesleyans, and a Baptist or Congregationalist wishing to enter Parliament under Tory auspices would receive encouragement from headquarters. So far, I believe, there is no evidence of any overwhelming desire to grasp at the proffered honour.

The whipping up of a sixpenny subscription to keep Mr. John Burns in public:life has resulted in the collection of .£197 49, It was neither a delicate nor a judicious course on the part of Mr. Burns's friends to adopt, and I never could understand why Mr. Burns permitted it to go on. If his constituents or his trades union did not sufficiently appreciate his exertions on behalf of labour and Radicalism to maintain him in Parliament, Mr. Burns could have retired with dignity,and with the respect of all who had watched his course in the House of Commons, to the more modest role he played before he was sent to the Imperial Palace of Talk at Westminster. There are only two methods of .work before the Labour leaders at present, and Mr. Burns may be said to follow the one and Mr. Keir Hardie the other. The one is to squeeze out of the Liberal party all that can possi- bly be got from it. This is Mr. Burns's course. The other is to form public opinion outside Parliament Rana view to strengthening the Labour party at the next election, and this is Mr. Keir Hardie's. While, Mr. Burns, therefore, has been a constant attender in Parliament, Mr. Hardie has been seldom in the house; but, either in the one case or the other, the labour world should see that the men who are fighting its battles should be in a position t.) do 80 with effect.

Sir Walter Raleigh wa; the first white7man to use maliocrany lumber. In the year 1595, while at Trinidad, he repaired one of his ships with a mahogany plank. That incident caused its introduction into England and into the commerce of the world.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 22, 1894.

The paper stockings now being manufactured in Ger- many are said to be a great preventive of colds. French papers announce that Miss Anna Gould, the late Jay Gould's daughter, is engaged to Prince Francis of Battenberg, the youngest brother of Prince Henry. He is a lieutenant of Hessian Infantry. Miss Goald's yearly income is estimated at nearly a quarter of a million.

Madame Patti has had so many tempting offers from abroad this winter that she has had great difficulty in deciding which to accept. At last she has signed con- tracts to sing a certain number of nights in opera at Nice shortly after Christmas, and later on to appear at concerts in Germany and Austria. Japanese papers say that the oldest married couple in that country live in Sawada, in the province of Sado. The man is one hundred and thirty-three years old and his wife one hundred and thirty-five. Of the faimily numbering fourteen persons, the eldest daughter is one, hundred and eight years old and the son one hundred and five.

From St. Petersburg comes the story of a charming young widow who raised a magnificent tomb over het late husband, and placed the following inscription 0(1 it :—" My grief is so great that I cannot bear it." Time however, assuaged her grief, and a year or two after- wards she married a handsome officer of the Imperial Guard. The inscription to her former husband at first troubled her a little, but she got over the difficulty by adding the word "alone." Miss Dora Russell, the well-known novelist, was born in Northumberland, and was reared in comfortable and happy circumstances, the" Queen "tells us. As a chil. she used to run up and down the garden repeating th( most romantic histories and pieces of poetry, wove( from her childish imagination. Trouble fell upon her family. One day she saw an advertisement in the" Ne- wcastle Chronicle" offering prizes for fiction and poetry She sent in MSS., and received two prizes. " The Miner's Oath "appeared in that paper. This introduce( her to Messrs. Routledge and Sons, who assisted hei greatly.

Distinguished women and their dolls have provided the material for an interesting article from the pen oi Miss Frances Low, the lady who wrote the famous des- cription of the Queen's dolls, which, by the way, has just been republished in book form, with coloured illus- trations. Miss Lowe has had the happy idea of ques- tioning famous women as to the place which dolls occu pied in their affections when they were children. The Empress Frederick seems to have inherited her Roya mother's tondness for dolls. Miss Braddon, the novelist confesses that she worked for her dolls until she was in her teens, and Mrs. Fawcett pleads guilty to a doll wor- ship of which one would hardly have dared suspret lady so absorbed in serious interests. This passion for dolls was, I believe, characteristic of Mrs. Fawcett': daughter, the Senior Wrangler, in her early youth.

Ladies and Cycling. The daughters of the Prince of Wales are skilful riders not on tricycles merely but on bicycles, but they have not yet ventured out into the open afforded by th, country roads round Sandringham or Balmoral. Yet if anyone could peep at times within the royal or princel3 domain, he would see the princesses acquitting them- selves with very great skill and credit, and with great enjoyment, on some of the private paths. Mrs. Asquith is also an accomplished rider, and, as might have been expected of so very dashing a horsewoman and so origi- nal a lady, she is a very daring one. She is beaten, however, by some other aristocratic lady riders, trrnong them being Lady de Grey,perhaps the most accomplisheo bicyclist in fashionable circles. When ladies of rank and social standing who are not remarkable for oddity or love of notoriety have taken with enthusiasm to bicycle riding as a pleasant and healthful recreation, we may expect soon to see a ladies' track and ladies' races as part of our ordinary experience; perhaps even to hear of lady champions breaking the record of some future Shorlansl ; and to meet not only with deans and deans' wives on the graceful wheel, but bishops themselves and their better halves riding tandems on their missions of religion and charity throughout their dioceses.

THE DOUBLE LIFE;

OR, THE HAMPTON MYSTERY..

By the Author of " DARKWOOD."

CHAPTER %XXIII. DEFIANCE. I had rather chop this hand off at a blow, And with the other fling it at thy face Than bear so low a sail, to strike to thee!

SHAKESPEARE.

As we have said, Lord Adlows burst into the drawing-room of The Evergreens with the resistless 'rush of a tornado.

Closing the door behind him, and locking it in the face of the frightened Meggy Fleck, he leaned against it, glaring around him like a tiger. The Lady Beatrice and the self-styled Spanish count arose from the sofa at his entrance—the former white with horror, the cry of terror still quivering on her lips, her manner gathering a desperate defiance, like some hunted creature turning at bay. The countenance of the latter was instinct with a haughty surprise and questioning sternness.

The two boys had retired. The cousins—rivals all their lives—bitter enemies sow—looked each other full in the face. Adlowe searched in vain those dark and un- familiar features for some sign of recognition or fear.

The wronged Geoffrey Trevelyan had anticipated too long such an hour as this not to be prepared for it. Despite the lapse of years since their last heeting, he recognised at once his cousin—recog- nised him with a chill foreboding, and a deadly sinking at the heart. Yet astonishment and anger at the singular intrusion were the only emotions expressed by his mobile and well-trained features. "What means this strange intrusion 1" he de- Manded, in a strong foreign accent, with haughti- ness of look and tone. Lord Adlowe looked from one to the other of the couple in sudden bewilderment and confusion. The looks, the manner, the speech, the appear- ance of Senor Arevalo were so unlike those of his wronged cousin, that Adlowe's faith in his identity received a sudden shock.

The Lady Beatrice, whose eyes were fixed upon him with an eager look of utter desperation, saw his lordship's face change, and marked the uneasy expression that came into his pale eyes. In an in- stant she began to recover her courage. Her man- ner grew decidedly defiant, and thoroughly self- possessed: You are a good actor, Geoffrey Trevelyan !" declared Adlowe, with a hard and bitter sneer. I never saw a better on the stage. But you cannot deceive me!"

The pretended count looked puzzled, but still surprised. Geoffrey Trevelyan ! " he repeated, with his strong assumed accent. "Be kind enough to speak plainly, Senor. Or, better, leave us. You are mad—intoxicated—"

Adlowe turned from the count with a gesture of disgust, fixing his venomous eyes on the Lady Beatrice.

"You, at least, make no attempt to mystify me!" he said, a demon's joy lighting up his face. "I have discovered your secret, Beatrice Hampton. I have probed to the bottom the strange mystery of your life. I know now the reason of your celi- bacy, so called—the cause of your mysterious se- elusions and absences. You a wife in secret! You a mother! What will your proud father and the gay world say to my discovery ?" A sudden emotion convulsed the perfect features of the Lady Beatrice. She put out her hand blindly and caught at the back of a chair, leaning heavily upon it. An instant later, however, she recovered herself, arched her haughty neck, and said coldly: "Do your worst, Lord Adlowe. I scorn and defy you 1" "And your father? And the world?" "I have long been prepared for such an hour as this," responded the Lady Beatrice, with haughty calmness. "In an hour I can be on my way to France."

"And leave your father, the poor old earl, to bear his trouble and tho sneers and jeers of people alone," said Lord Adlowe, tauntingly. "And your Spanish Sener—will he go, too?" "He will go where I go," said the Lady Bea- trice, firmly. "Lord Adlowe, Senor Arevalo is my husband. The boys whom you saw through the window are mine. I have been married many years. You have surprised my secret in a manner appropriate to your character, base spy that you are. Now, as I said before, do your worst." "My worst ! " repeated Adlowe, significantly. "Your very worst! Proclaim to the world that I have been for many years a wife and mother, while passing for an unmarried woman! Break down my father's pride—humble him to the very dust—break his heart if you will," and her voice trembled suddenly, "but know that you will fail in all your dearest schemes! You will never bumble or break the heart of Beatrice Hampton 1 'You cannot rob her of her husband's love or of her boys! And if you can find one stain on the name of Antonio Arevalo, proclaim that also. . As to me," she added, "I do not find my happiness in society. I should be supremely contented to live with my dear ones in some foreign land, and never see England again, since it is not in the country, but in my home I find my happiness!" "Indeed !" said Adlowe, with an intonation of wonder, for he could not disassociate in his thoughts the Lady Beatrice from the society where she had so long reigned a star and queen. You will corns out in a new character, then. You a model wife and mother. You a homeheeper," and he sneered. "As well look for an eagle in a dove's nest 1 But about the 'Senor 2' If we can find no stain on the name of 'Antonio Arevalo,' we shall find a pretty large one on that of Geoffrey Tre- velyan. ' "That name again !" said the pretended count, Impatiently. "What have Ito do with your friend ?"

"My enemy !" corrected Adlowe, his eyes flaming with sudden hatred. ' "My enemy to the death! The man who stole from me the love of the woman for whom I have waited these eighteen years! The man who lives to come between me and a noble inheritance for which I have striven hard all my life long! Oh, curse him 1" and he ground his teeth savagely, and glared at the hus- band of the Lady Beatrice with a look of concen- trated fury and hatred. "The base robber! The cowardly intending assassin of the uncle who loved him!"

The pretended count faced Adlowe with an air of command, flashes of indignant light leaping from the blue eyes under his dark brows. His countenance grew strangely stern, and he exclaimed haughtily: "Have you done? Spy and madman that you are Leave my house! Leave it at once, or I will expel you with my own hands ! " A cool and insolent smile chased the anxious ex- pression from Adlowe's features. "It would be the worst day's work you ever did, Geoffrey Trevelyan, if you succeeded in expelling me," he said, significantly. " Come ; I know you, despite your clever disguise. I know you by the fact that the Lady Beatrice Hampton would have wedded no other than her early love." The count uttered no disclaimer, but a smile thstpuzzled the intruder played for a moment about his bearded lips. Adlowe moved uneasily, and communed with himself, keeping a watchful eye upon his antago- nists.

The Lady Beatrice drew nelrer to her husband, who smiled upon her, and gently wreathed his arm around her waist. He felt her heart fluttering like a frightened bird in its cage, although outwardly she seemed so resolute and defiant.

The manner of Adlowe, which grew every instant more menacing and insolent, began to inspire them with a growing apprehension. The count swept a rapid glance at the windows in search of hostile faces. The idea occurred to him that Adlowe might have officers in waiting without to arrest him.

The Lady Beatrice noticed her husband's glance, and comprehended his unspoken fear. Quitting his encouraging embrace she went to the window, opened it, and koked out. There was no one to be seen in the shadowy garden. Her fears quieted, she left the window open and returned to her hus- band's side.

"I came here alone," said Adlowe, comprehend- ing her ladyship's movement. "A detective in my employ traced the Lady Beatrice here. I have a key to your garden gate, and admitted myself. No one but the detective suspects that I am here, or that Senor Arevalo is no other than Geoffrey Trevelyan. My uncle does not dream that his hated nephew still lives. Now let us understand each other. I believe you, Senor Arevalo, as you call yourself, to be my cousin Geoffrey Trevelyan. The fact is plain to my own mind. If I were to denounce you to my uncle, he would transport you. You would die of the shame . and disgrace, but you would leave sons to inherit your title and estates. You see that I recognise all the obstacles between me and the property I lave e_o lop_g e_onsisie_red _as_ my own, - AAA now 2!

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE, Everyone is at the seaside or holiday-keeping In some form or another just now, and numbers of fresh girl faces, all the fresher for sea breezes blowing vigorously inland, are to be met at every turn on beach, parade, and pier. Somehow, girls seem to dress better nowadays than they did formerly. I cannot help thinking it is because, week by week, more and more clever heads dis- cover tEat the way to dress well on a small allow- ance is for clever fingers to make the dresses, frills, and furbelows at home instead of giving them to a, perhaps, inexperienced drestmaker.

The pretty frocks wb, tave seen at a fashion- able resort are, besides being charming dresses for the season, excellent models to follow when you are taking autumn and winter dresses into con- sideration. The girl's frock is serge, the skirt, yoke, and lower part of the sleeves white, striped with red, the Eton coat, puffs, and blouse plain red, and the collar white, braided with black; the anchor is also worked in black silk. Black stock- ings are worn with this and tan shoes, the straw of the hat is in a corresponding tone of tan, and the ribbon on it black, with red letters and red anchors. Her elder sister is very modishly attired; a creamy shade of serge is the chief material of which the gown is composed, the accordion-pleated bodice being made of thin black silk, with cream guipure revers and flounce. Black satin ribbons cross from the revers to each side of the waist, and the three long sash ends are deftly caught at the edge of the skirt. The hat is a burnt straw, with black tulle and orchids.

Among the new trimmings arranged for autumn wear, are regular sets in astrachan, including revers, collar, cuffs, and jacket trimmings. Sometimes the sets are for bodices, and are devised with a view to render them wearable without a coat or cape in mid-season. For instance, a sort of zouave or bolero in astrachan is edged all round with ball fringe, and with it are sold a high collar and a long pair of cuffs, which can be buttonod on ever the dress collar and sleeves. It is certain that beaver and astrachan will be the favourite furs this winter. It also seems pretty sure that skirts will continue to be very wide round the hem, though the fit to the figure above is still regarded as one of the most important points of a smart toilet. Some dressmakers still use a stiff lining in order to make the folds stand out well from the feet at the back. The new skirts with pleats dawn the sides are well suited to redundant figures, for though they add to the quantity of material on the hips, the spreading out of the pleats immediately below them detracts from the effects of stoutness. It will be seen if these skirts will be more success- ful with Englishwomen than the draped ones, which some French milliners vainly endeavoured to Introduce.

Directly the wind begins to -blow we shall cast aside from us our larQe hats, and take again to the toque. Made in coloured velvet, edged with sable, with an eagle's quilt e.t one side, these are particu- larly pretty; and then, again, the crowns of some of them are of felt, ret,ting on folds of velvet; and others, &gait:, I,ave I Ines with crowns of Indian embtoidery outlined with R ruche of cocks feathers, with cock's feathers stic-king up at one kiie but, unquestionably, the velvet ones with the sable are the prettiest, and sal-As is once agsin to be the fur. A pr,sttiE,,r iat for a you:lg nirl cannot well be created than one made of ertuklecl straw, trimmed with lace and white ostrich feathers. Under and slightly rahiui.! the brim are two tufts of dydrangea of a delicate pinkish shade. Yours lovingly, JENNIE.

A MN WHO WANTED TO DIE WITH HIS LEG ON.

The " Oldham Chronicle "prints an interesting inter- view with Mr. John Cavanagh, of 13, Slater-street, Man- chester-street, Oldham. " Four years ago," said Mr. Cavanagh, who is des cubed as a most intelligent person, "I slipped down some steps. Next morning I could not go to my work. The pain settled in my risht knee. It began to swell, and a few mornings later I could not put my heel on the ground, and that was my condition for four years. I went to the Oldham Infirmary as an out-patient; two of the best doctors saw me in consulta- tion. They said my leg was mortifying and that it would have to come off. I did not like that; I VMS determined I would die with my leg on. Other medical gentleman were called in, and I have been attended by no less than twenty-two ; eight of them entirely gave me up. After I had been helpless two years I went to the Infirmary. I shall never forget the look of astonish- ment on the doctor's face. He cried out Why the man is dying. Get him to bed.' I lay for sixteen days, daily getting worse. I was not out of bed for six months. One of the doe tors who saw me about this time sent eight others, who were equally puzzled with my case."

"You seem all right now," said the reporter, " how- ever. How does that come about ?"

" I came across accounts of cures effected by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. In December last I came across a report of a cure in Stallordshire,and the complaint appeared to be something like what I was suffering from. It was verified by a clergyman. I obtained a bow of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. It was on the 23rd of December, and so rapid was the effect of the medicine that I was actually working on the 4th of Jan- uary, for the first time for four years, and have worked regularly ever since. lam as well as ever I was in my life ; I can run, jump, or skip."

Dr. Willi am-Pink.Pills are not a purgative, like othe pills, but a strengthening nerve and blood tonic which can be taken without the least danger even by the most delicate, and are an unfailing cure for rheumatism, sciatica, neuralgia, partial paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, prostration, and all cases in which the system needs to be strengthened and built up. They are a specific for troubles of the female sex, and in men effect a radical cure in cases arising from mental worry, overwork, or excess of any kind. They are sold by chemists, or may be bad direct from the Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46, Holborn-viaduct, London, at '2s. 9th a box, or six boxes for 135.. 9d. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are new r sold loose, and the wooden box must be in a pitk wrapper, bearing full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

MR GLADSTONE AND THE LIQUOR TRAFFIC.

The Bishop of Ches ter addressed a meeting in Aber- deen on Tuesday night on the subject of the municipal- isation of the drink traffic. His lordship discussed the Gothenburg system of licensing, and read the following hitherto unpublished letter which Mr. Gladstone had recently addressed to Lord Thring :—" For many years I have been strongly of opinion that the principle of selling liquors for the public profit only offers the sole chance of escape from the present miserable and almost contemptible pred ica merit, which is a disgrace to the country. lam friend ly to local option, but it can be no more than a partial and occasional remedy. The mere limitation of numbers, the Ideal of Parliament for the last 20 years, is, though pretending to the honour of a remedy, little better than an imposture. The growth of the system of tied houses continually aggravates the prevailing mischief of dethils. Ido not speak, but on a principle. You are working on the only lines either promising or tenable. lam glad to see that Mr. Cha- mberlain is active in your cause."

The Sailors and Firemen's Union claims to have scored a victory. According to Mr. J. Havelock Wilson, M.P., 1,368 men shipped in the Bristol Channel in the last two weeks at the increased wages demanded by the union. It is stated the union proposes to make a similar demand at other ports. Mr. E. Cowey, president of the Yorkshire Miners' Association, was waited upon on Monday by a deputa- tion from a meeting held E t Penistone on Saturday, and accepted an invitation to become the Labour candidate for the Holmfirth division, in opposition to Mr. H. J. Wilson, M.P.,the Liberal member tor the division, whose action on the Mines Eight Hours Bill has alienated the support of a portion of bis constituents. .

he added, turning his bloodshot eyes front Mae to the other of his enemies and victims, "I offer you a truce. I will make a bargain with you. Which shall it be between us; peace or war—a war to the death?"

The count was gravely silent, but his eyes seemed to read the soul of the scheming profligate before him.

"How can there be peace between us?" asked the Lady Beatrice, suspiciously. "Can the lion and the lamb lie down together ? " "I will explain," declared Adlowe. "Granting that Senor Arevalo is my cousin Geoffrey--I say granting it, Lady Beatrice—you know that he must keep his existence hidden from my uncle during Lord Trevelyan's lifetime. After his lord- ship's death Geoffrey can come forward and claim his own. I will keep Geoffrey's continued existence a secret, granting as I said that Senor Arevalo is Geoffrey, and I will undertake to disabuse my uncle's mind of its prejudices—for a considera- tion."

"What consideration ? " asked the Lady Bea- trice, guardedly. Adlowe shuffled a little as if uneasy, smiled and said:

"You have a daughter?" The father started and his brows contracted. A look of gathering terror appeared on the face of the lady Beatrice. __ _ __ _ _

" I iefer to Giralda," said the profligate lord, coolly. "I have but just returned from my uncle's where I have seen her. Sha ha her father's eyes. You sent her to the Park to win the old man's love, and to smooth the way for her father's return. Well, she has succeeded in winning Lord Trevelyan's love and all the money he is able to leave from the estate. The Park looks as it used to, with servants and a better style of living than it has seen since the row of eighteen years ago. Giralda has it all her own way there. But to come to the point. Your daughter is young, spirited, lovely in disposi- tion, with a beauty that is perfectly marvellous. I never saw a more bewitching face in all my travels. She is like her mother, but with less of coldness and haughtiness, and more of fire and glow." "Well " demanded the pretended count, hoarsely. The Ladyßeatrice held closely to the back of the chair before her, a deathly faintness creeping over her.

"Since the Lady Beatrice is lost to me by her marriage," said Adlowe, easily, "I will elevate her lovely daughter to the pedestal in my heart which her ladyship has vacated. In other words, give me Giralda for my wife and I will keep your secret, and endeavour to effect Geoffrey's reconciliation with my uncle. But understand, I must be recom- pensed for the estates of which Geoffrey's existence robs me. Lord Trevelyan's sixty thousand pounds settled on Giralda must be increased by a full half of the private fortune of the Lady Beatrice. On these terms I agree to a lasting peace." He looked up as if he felt himself master of the situation.

The Lady Beatrice uttered a low moan, and put out her hands towards her husband.

He took them both in his strong grasp, looking brave and undaunted, and said quietly, but with a dignity and decision that were more effective than loud tones:

"My lord, I beg to decline the alliance you pro- pose to us. My daughter is too young to marry. Anti, were she not, I should never attempt to force her inclinations."

"Oh, I would see her in her grave before I would see her Lord Adlowe's wife! "cried the Lady Bea- trice with passionate emphasis. "My poor, little girl! My noble, pure Giralda 1" A disagreeable smile curved Adlowe's mouth. "You prefer war, then ?" he said. "But do not decide yet. I have more to tell. I have been down to Trevelyan Park, as I said. In fact, I left there last night, arriving in town this morning. Yesterday, not being then informed of the detec- tive's discovery, and making sure of Giralda's identity, I offered myself to her, and assured her that her marriage with me would be her father's only chance of safety." "What did she say? "questioned the Lady Bea- trice, breathlessly. "She put on the airs of a duchess, and refused me 1 She said that she could not sacrifice herself, could not do wrong, even to save her father 1" " Nobld) girl 1" the father muttered, under his breath, his face lighting up with a thankful glow. "That is not all 1" said the mother, anxiously, "What else have you to tell, Lord Adlowe 1" That disagreeable smile on Adlowe's lips deep- ened now to one of triumph. "Your daughter," he said, "had formed the project of stealing away from the Park after night- fall, intending to walk to Trevelyan village and to catch the evening train east She also intended to telegraph a warning to you. I read her resolve in her eyes. As I was leaving her, I received the detective's dispatch, hurrying me back to town. My course was settled upon at once. I procured a light wagon from the village, and had it in readi- ness. Soon after dusk. according to my calcula- tions, Miss Giralda stole out, on her way to the village. I and my man followed with the waggon. We overtook her on the road, and found her crouching by a tree or stone, where she hid at our approach— `My poor girl I" cried the Lady Beatrice, tor. tured and anguished. A gleam shot from Adlowe's eyes. "We stopped the waggon and took the young lady prisoner," he resumed, coolly. "I bouna her to the seat and sent her away to a safe asylum. She is there now, aprisoner." "Oh, my God I', wailed the stricken mother, "Better that she were in the den of a lion than in your power' Ormond Adlowe." She sank down on the sofa, quivering in frame, her senses in a whirl.

The disguised Geoffrey Trevelyan looked stunned by this unexpected blow. "The price of Giralda's freedom is her marriage with me," said Adlowe, remorselessly. "She shall never leave her living tomb save as my wife. You must see for yourself that this marriage will be the best thing for her. She will never have another chance to secure a respectable husband! You are versed in the cruel ways of the world, Lady Beatrice. You know that I have only to circulate the story of Giralda's mysterious disap- pearance, to blight her fair name for ever. People are quick to believe bad reports. And so, if you prefer war, you may add to Geoffrey's threatened transportation your daughter's blighted name and blighted life !" He looked at them now, smiling like a fiend. Again a low wail broke from the mother's lips. The words of Adlowe had fallen upon her quivering soul like heavy whip-strokes upon raw and bleeding flesh.

The face of the pretended count glowed with an awful indignation. His eyes flashed like gleaming coals. His breath came quick through his parted lips. 'Scoundrel V' he cried, leaping forward, carried away by his passion. "You shall never go further in your demon's work 1" He sprang upon Adlowe seizing him in a fierce grip, as if he would tear him in pieces. Adlowe shook in his frenzied grasp, unable to withstand him, and uttered a cry of terror. The Lady Beatrice bounded forward and caught her husband's arm. "Oh, stop 1" she exclaimed in anguish. "Our child 1 The secret of her prison will die with him! Adlowe, where is she? Where is Giralda ?" The count relaxed his grasp, and questioned the profligate lord with haggard eyes. "Where is she?" he whispered, hollowly, shak- ing Adlowe as if he had been a dog. "Where is she?"

_ "That you shall know when I can present her to you as my wife—not before I" declared Adlowe, with chattering teeth, his cowardly soul quailing before the storm he had raised. "Kill me, if you will, Geoffrey Trevelyan, but with me will die your last chance of ever beholding your child again!' The count half unconsciously shook Adlowe again, and more fiercely. "I ought to kill you, Ormond Adlowe !" he cried, his eyes blazing. "Wretch that you are, your life has been one long series of wrong-doing! Viper! let me tell you who I am !" and he gripped him more tightly. "I am Geoffrey Trevelyan— that Geoffrey Trevelyan whom you have wronged and traduced! I am that Geoffrey Trevelyan against whom you have alienated his uncle's heart ; whom you have driven from his rightful home; whom you have forced to lead a secret and hidden existence under an assumed name and assumed character! You have persecuted my noble and honoured wife. You have persecuted my daughter. And at last, Ormond Adlowe, at last we meet face to face, disguises swept aside !" He flung Adlowe from him violently, and con- fronted him with a face of awful sterness. "I am Geoffrey Trevelyan !', he repeated, the concentrated bitterness of years finding expression in his voice. lt ITN CORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Mr. John Mclntyre presided over the fortnightly meeting of guardians at the Dutton Workhouse, on Tuesday afternoon. The other gentlemen present were the Rev. J. W. N. Tanner, Messrs. H. E. Hey- wood, J. Buindreth, J. M. Williams, T. Baxter, C.C. T. Handley, T. Lea, S, Yarwood, T. Lewisa, W. Whit- low, S. Woodward, E. Aaron, W. Turner, J. Ashton, clerk; and G. Ashton, deputy clerk.

REFORMATION OF THE LAUNDRY AND WASHHOIISE.

At the last meeting of the Baard, a specification and estimate by Messrs. Bradford and Company, tor appli- ances for the washhouse and laundry at the workhouse. amounting in the aggregate to £196 103. was submitted by the Visiting Committee. They reported that they had carefully considered the matter, and recommended that so much of the work as would entail an expenditure ,n the purchase of appliances of £147, and in the altera- tions and additions consequen t thereupon of about £5O he executed. Mr. Williams then gave notice that at the next meeting of the Board he would mOve that such re- port be received and adopted, and that applieation be made to the Local Government Board for sanction to carry out the work.—Mr. Williams in accordance with that notice of motion now proposed that the above work be done. The sub-committee had come to the conclusion after considerable investigation, to spend this money in replacing certain appliances and making al: ers.tions which would only be humane on their part towards:, the old psi p!e who were obliged to work in the washficuses and heating room. The present appliances had now been in use for nearly forty years, and were nearly worn out. The Ladies Committee had reported that the laundry, &e., were in an unsatisfactory state,and Mr. Dauser had also spoken of the need for improvement, anti had sug- gested various alterations and additions. The cost would be 1-5 of a id. in the £ on their present rateable valuo,or if the sum was borrowed for ten years, it would. mean a payment of £2l a year to cover principal and in- terest.—The Rev.J.W.N.Tanner seconded and Mr. Aaron supported.—Mr. Yarwood remarked that he felt in duty bound to oppose the spending of so much money at a time when trade was so depressed. Toe sum was too large to spend upon the laundry when the necessary im- , provements could be carried out at a cost of about £6O or £7O. He thought that the proposed expenditure would prove a very heavy burden on the backs of the ratepayers ; and it behoved the Board to therefore be just before they were generous. He sympathised with the old folks who were compelled to work under such conditions in the laundry, but nevertheless he was of the opinion that the place could be made very comfort- able for about £7O or at the most £lOO. He would move anamendment therefore to that effect.—Mr. Whitlow seconded.—Mr. Woodward said that this work had been before two committees both of whom were fully satisfied that the improvements,as now proposed, were at tsolutely necessary. It was a graver mistake to patch work of this kind than to spend a few more pounds and do the work properly.—Mr. Brandreth observed that all the members of the committee were practical men and need- less to say they had tried to get the work done at the lowest possible figure, but they found that the present estimate could not be cut down without leaving some of the work undone or improperly done.—Mr. Heywood and Mr. Lea also supported the motioo.—The amend- ment was at length put and lost,and the original motion carried by nine votes to two.—On the proposition of Mr. Heywood, seconded by Mr. Lea, it was resolved that the consent of the Local Government Board be applied for to borrow the money for a isumber of years.

THE YEAR'S POOR RATE RETURN.

The Clerk presented the poor rate return for the year ended Lady Day,1894. The total receipts wt r, £24,222, of which £21,730 was raised by the poor rate. The expenditure for relief to the ,poor and oth...r relative purposes amounted to £8,002, made up as follows :—ln maintenance, £1,172 ; out relief, £3,557 ; lunatics in asylums, £823 ; on loans, £219; salaries, &c., £1,339 ; other expenses, £892. The expenditure tor purposes unconnected with relief was :—For county pglice rate, £6 853 ; highways, £4,556 ; burial boards, £145 ; Rural Sanitary authorities, £1,193 ; school board, £1,862 ; School Attendance Cotnmittee,£lss ; registration ,births, &c., £159 ; vaccination fees and expenses, £152 ; regis- tration and cost of jury lists, £lB9 19s. ; school tees for non-pauper children, £ll ; legal proceedings, £lB ; assessment expenses, £ll5 ; salaries, £1,336 ; total ex- penditure charged to common fund,£24,765. The loans raised by the Guardians stood at £1,520. The total medical relief was £373.

A VISIT TO UPTON LUNATIC ASYLUM.

The Rev. J. W. N. Tanner submitted the report of the Committee, consisting ot himself, and Messrs. Y3rwood, Lewis, and J. Ashton (clerk), who had been appointed to visit Upton Lunatic Asylum. The rev. gentleman stated that the visit was made on the 11th inst.. the Committee being very cordially received by the asylum authorities. In th- building were 38 patients chargeable to the Run- corn Union, the cost of each being 7s. per week, 4,4. of which was repaid to them by the County Council. They were made up of nineteen males and nineteen females, a- decrease of three compared with last year. All these cases were quite hopeless as was very apparent to the committee when they questioned the effected persons. They were all very clean and well-treated by the officials.—Mr. Yarwood endorsed the remarks of the Isit speaker. All the patients seemed to I e well cared for.—Mr. Williams asked if the Union did not have any patients in Parkside Asylum.—The Chairman : Yes. We have two.—Mr. Williams said that as nothing was ever heard or seen of them, it would be well to have them removed to Upton.—The Chairman replied that there was no room at Uptou. All the beds were full.— Mr. Lewis seconded the adoption of the report and it was carried.

THE PUBLIC-HOUSE MANAGER QUESTION.

MESSRS. WALKER'S CREWE HOUSES.

An adjourned licensing session was held at Cre,ve on Tuesday,the Mayor presiding, when the question of renewing the ligences of seven houses belonging to Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, Limited, hitherto conducted by managers, came on for decision. Last year the magistrates refused to renew except to bona-fide tenants. Messrs. Walker appealed to the Cheshire Quarter Sessions, which reversed the deci- sion, and renewed the licences. This year, at the ordinary renewal session, the magistrates repeated their objection, and declined to be ruled by the decision of the sessions, and the cases were ad- joured to Tuesday for Messrs. Walker to decide on their course of action. Mr. Berry, for Messrs. Walker, now said his clients, out of deference to the wishes of the Bench, had made their 'managers into tenants, and produced agreements to that effect. The Mayor,after the documents had been examined, said the Bench were not altogether satisfied with the terms of the agreements, but on this occasion they would grant the applications. He said the view of the bench was that licenced houses should be held by responsible persons baying substantial pecuniary interests at stake.

PEARSON AND KNOWLES COAL AND

IRON CO., LIMITED.

The 21st report of the directors of the above named company for the year ending June 30 has just been issued, and is as follows :—The profits for the year end- ing June 30 last amount to £13,485 6s. 5d., and the bal- ance brought forward from last year is £5423 53. 6c1., together £18,908 lls. 11d. The directors recommend that the preference dividend at the rate of 6 per cent. per annum bepaid on the A shares for the half year end- ing December 31. 1893, but the present state of the accounts does not in their opinion warrant any further distribution. The directors also recommend that £5OOO be added to the depreciation fund ,and that the balance, £7581 13.3. 9d., be carried forward to the next account. The collier's strike, which extended over one-third of the whole financial year, has most seriously affected tne company's business in all departments, and as a conse- quence very heavy losses have had to be made good. In addition to this, the state of the iron trade has throughout the year been extremely depressed and un- satisfactory, and at present no improvement is percep- tible.

'I E EAMrNEJ, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 22 1894.

TECHNICAL INSTRUCTION IN WARRINGTON. DISTRIBUTION OF PRIZES. The distribution of prizes to the successful students belonging to the Warrington Technical Institute took place at the People's College on Mon- day evening. There was a large number of past and present students present and also Mr.T. Morris, F.U.S. (who distributed the prizes),Councillors Shaw Green, tied F. W. Monks, and Meesrs. C. Twies, E. Wallington, and C. Madeley (secretary). Councillor SHAW GREEN,in opening the proceedings,said that he was sorry that the Chairman of the Museum, Library, and Technical Instruction Committee was not able to be present. In his a hsenc.e,they must not longer delay the preceedings, and he would at once ask Mr. Madeley to read the annual report. Mr. MAPELEY read the following report :—The Con- tinution School was established and carried on for one winter by a voluntary committee. On taking it over the Museum and Technical Instruction Committee made some additions to the list of subjects taught with the view of increasing the use of the school in preparing students for the commercial and scientific departments of the school of science, cookery was also added, and the school was opened four nights instead of three, weekly. The school having become better known there was an increase in the number of students who joined, the total re:iching 197, against 146 in the previous session. The number who entered for each subject was as follows—Aril hmetic and mensuration 90, writing and composition, 70, English grammar 53, shorthands4l, drawing 37, mechanics 13, chemistry 65, needlework 16, cookery 19, total 404. The average number of attend- ances made per week was 393, about one-third more than in the previous session. Regular work being obvi- ously most essential to the progress of the student and very properly insisted on by the Education Department, the committee offered prizes on the following terms : To each student who made 80 attendances a book prize (first class) and to each who made 50 attendances a book prize (second class). In the result 50 students became entitled to these prizes, which Mr. Morris had kindly , undertaken to distribute that evening, viz., 33 first class and 17 second class. As a further reward for industry and intelligence the committee offered scholarships of an amount sufficient to cover class fees in the next seesien. These scholarships were given on the result of the examinations held by the Union of Lancashire and , Cheshire Institutes in the spring. Thirteen were granted, and as the winners have received notice, there is nothing more to give them until the end of the session I will reed their names now : Mary Carpenter, a scholar- ship value 104. tenable at the School of Science ;Jowph Barton, Marshall Gende, Thomas Gandy, Edith Gill, Herbert Greene, Sarah Horobin, Thomas Loneehaw, Florence M'Garty, William Parker, Susannah Penny, scholarships value 7s. 61., covering the whole of the Continuation School and the elites in tte School of Science ; Annie Basketer, Ella London, scholarships of ss. at the Continuation School. These scholarships are given like those in the School of Science on condition that the holder joins the school again, pays the fees, and attends regularly during the next session. It is very satisfactory to the committee, and will also he satisfac- tory to the students, to know that in the opinion of the Educetion Department of the Government this school is It good school. We were visited by her Majesty's Inspeetors several times last session ,when they examined into all our methods and proceedings. As a result of their report the Government has allowed the highest grant for all the subjects taught in the school, the Science and Art Department doing the same in regard to the drawing, and the inspector's report says, " The school has been efficiently conducted and given excellent preparation for pupils passing to the Technical School." This result has not been brought about without some hard work, and the committee are indebted to the super- intendent, Mr. 11. J. Hall, and the other teachers of the school for their exertions towards this end. Councillor SHAW GREEN observed that it was very gratifying to see such a large assembly in that room that night. When the technical schools were first inaugurated some doubts existed as to whether they would be success- ful. He thought that they had been an assured success. At the opening night last year they had 80 students, and 197 during the whole course of the session. That night, more than 150 had already given in their names for the present year, and that was a gratifying feature indeed. (Applause.) He hoped that the names handed in would go on increasing, and that the students would make themselves more efficient, and then at the close of next sessien. they would have the pleasure, not?! distribut- ing 50 prizes, but 150. It would be gratifying to the Committee if they had to find the money, and he hoped everyone of them would make a special effort to be owners of a prize next session. Applause.) Mr. T. MORRIS in his remarks previous to distributing the prizes said that he stood before them with feelings mixed with pain and pleasure. The rain was caused by the melancholy circumstances which caused their friend, Mr. Robert Davies, to he away from them that evening. The pleasure he needed hardly say was that he and his family, and his late brother's family had the sympathy of all in that room. (Rear, hear.) He was also pleased at the honour conferred upon him by being invited to present the prizes to the successful students that evening. By handing the prizes to the winners, he desired to impress upon all of them that they must not run away with the idea that when they won prizes or first class certificates, their education was finished— (hear, hear)—even in the particular subject for which they had received those prizes. It was at that stage that their real education was commencing—in the application of that education which would make them into men and women of worth. They should never allow the thriieht to stay in their brain, that all the things which tended to the happiness of the people were found nut, that the footpaths were altogether onen, and the road way clear. The roads were continually taxing borough surveyors and scientiflc men how they should find out some material which should he cheaper and as durable, or more durable and cheaper. At the same time that material should be as safe for man and horse to travel over. He wished to advise them all to com- mence to cultivate an inquiring mind. Never mind how simple or common an object Was. It was thus that George Stevenson acquired the idea of the locomotive, which did not occur to him until he was 25 years of are. Again Mr. Bessemer, now Sir Henry Bessemer, did not make his discoveries until he was 30 years of age. The gold in fact was not picked by some ()nth they were well on in years, therefore he entreated of them to keep on learning something which they and their parents might be proud of. If it did not bring wealth, it would bring them pleasure. (Hear, hear.) Pleasure meant health, and healeh was quite a. good as wealth. He was pleased at the success of the classes. If they kept multiplying, they would he the grandest things that Warrington or its people had even had in its hieorr. (Apt-dance.) Mr. MORRIS then handed the prizes to the successful students who numbered 50. The prizes consisted of books upon travel, science, etc. The following were the prize winners : s'econd prizes, Elizabeth Basketer, Robert Carpenter, Harry Gibson. Joseph Gibson, Annie Houghton. Emily flonehton, Herbert Johnson, Lilian Lawrence. Walter Needham, Frank Owen, Arthur Pen- nington, Flareld Pennington, Thomas • Seed, Albert Spruce, Wm. Warburton. John Wilcox, Rueben Haigh ; second prize and certificate. The following were awsrded certificates : W. R. Barnes, Francis Herbert Cooper, John L. Hawthorn, F. J. MeGartv, Edward Summerfield, Edith Warburton. The follow- ing carried off first prizes :—Annie Arnold, Jos. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

CHEAP CORN.

At Spalding, which is the centre of an important corn-growing district, the prices for new corn were on Tuesday such as have not been known for a long period of years. For the finest wheat net more than 20s. ner quarter could be obtained. Many lots sold at 18s., whilst for rough samples the highest offer was 17s. Mer- chants described the trade as in a state of collapse. Oats were equally low-13s. to 16s. per quarter, and barley only made 18s. to 255. All round prices are most dis- heartening to growers, and the yield does not come up to expectations.

The largest" loran "or swaying stone is at Penims. Sicily. It weighs about three hundred and thirteen tons, and is so nicely balanced that a man can easily put it in motion.

THE REV. J. W. VARLEY'S CALL.

On Sunday night at the Baptish Chapel, Latchford, the Rev. J. W. Varley, pastor, informed his congregation that he had decided to accept the call from the Baptist Church at St. Anne's-on-the-Sea. De said that a fort- night ago he promised to make a further statement in connection with his position as pastor of that church. He had done his best to come to what he trusted was a right decision. He had asked himself the question,w hat was the will of God concerning his position as pastor of that church? He had tried to look at the circumstances .on both sides. On the one side there had been a people there with whom he had laboured so many years, and laboured as he believed with some degree of success. He had no hesitation in saying as he came to contem- plate the various members of that church that in his difficult position he had their complete sympathy, and had also •no hesitation in thinking that his ministry amongst them had been one that had been honoured by the fruitful ingathering of very many precious souls. When be came to look on the other side it seemed to him a distinct call of God. Well, he had to give them hip decision—a decision which would give pain and dis- appointment to the people belonging to that place—he considered that it was his duty to go. He asked them not to forget that the field of God was the world, and that if they were working in one part of his vineyard,he also would be working in another part, and he prayed that when he should leave the ministry in that place that God would send to them some brother that should do His work much more effectually than he had done. He had decided that that day five weeks he should close his ministry at that church.

===== 0002601_18940922_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RECTOR AND ROMAN CATHOLICISM.

The Rector (Rev. F. W. Willis) selected as his text the words "One body, one faith," Ephesians iv., 4 and 5, at his sermon at the Parish Church on Sun- day evening. Be said that they would know this was Ember week, one of the weeks appointed by the Church for praying and fasting for those about to be ordained. In view of that, and in view of the utter- ances which had lately been made in the neighbourhood —utterances which he thought bad surprised and shocked and disgusted all intelligent and instructed English Churchmen—he should make no apology for speaking as strongly as possible on those words. Referring to the various definitions of the" one body," he said the Non- conformist view was that the one body consisted of all who loved the Lord Jesus Christ in sincerity. That was a. beautiful definition but it was modern, it was unnat- ural because it took no view of the body, and it was unscriptura.l. Then the Roman view was that the body consisted of all those who were in union with, and in obedience to, the Pope. First of all, that view was opposed to the whole of the Eastern Church, which held that the chief heresy was the Papacy. Secondly, it was unscriptural. They knew that the basis given by the Romans was founded on St. Matthew xvi., 18, " And I say also unto thee, that thou art Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church," but he asked if it had never struck them as being worth noticing, that whereas 20 Fathers were in favour of the Roman view, yet they themselves were divided, and 40 Fathers were against it. The Apostolic and Scriptural view was that the one body consisted of the whole company of the baptised, that was those living and departed, and that the only one head of the Church was Christ. In this Epistle, if not before, St. Paul realised the grandeur of this—one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one Father of us all, and consist- ently with this he held the view which they held, of various branches, as for instance the branch of the Church of Corinth or the Church of Rome. So, in accordance with such teaching,they held that the Church in this country was the Natiisnal Church and a branch of the one Holy Catholic Church. He pointed out that a branch of this Catholic Church was founded in this country at the end of the first century. Whether its origin was from the east or the west they could not say with certainty. The liturgies pointed to an eastern origin, but the map would point to a western origin, while the natural communication would be from Jerusalem, through Rome, across the Continent. There was a cessation of intercommunion between the Church of this country and the Continent and the east and west for 150 years, that was from 450 to 600. Then in 600, with the coming of Augustine was reunion again, and a continuation of that union for a thousand years, till the years 1531 and 1534, and during that time arose the idea of the Papal supremacy. There was a final breach in the years 1531 and 1534. There was an utter rejection of the idea of Papal supremacy, and there was the un- fortunate concession of the clergy, putting Henry VIII. in the position of head upon earth,but with a reservation in favour of Christ so far as the law of Christ per- mitted. When Mary came to the throne in 1553, she deposed the bishops of Henry's reign and Edward's reign who preceded her—deposed them without any authority for it. When Queen Elizabeth came to the throne in 1559, the first great event of the Church was the consecration of Archbishop Parker by the bishops who had been deprived by Mary. They were true bishops,but they were without sees,hav- ing been deprived by Mary. Turning to the ques- tion of one faith, the Rector said there were certain doc- trines which he wished to speak to them particularly about. There were doctrines which were common to

Nonconformists. Romans and themselves, as for instance the Trinity, Incarnation, the atonement, the divinity and personality of the Holy Ghost. There were other doctrines which were distinctively Roman, as for in- stance, the assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary, the Immaculate conception, the Papal sapremacy,and Papal infallibility. These were distinctly Roman and not Catholic. Then there were certain other Catholic doctrines which were not understood and not appreciated by many who called them- selves Churchmen, as for instance—and he noticed it because this was Ember week—the ministry, the ordina- tion of the Priesthood. It might be said that, the word priest was simply a contracti,an and shortening of the word presbyters. So it was, but their authority as priests did not depend upon that, but upon their Lord. The Rector proceeded to explain the nature of the true Catholic doctrines, and in closing urged them, as it was Ember week, to consider carefully during the week the creeds and the Catechism and the Ordinal, and pray to God for those about to be ordained.

REVIS[ON COURT.

WIDNES.

On Monday morning, at Widnes Revision Court, held at the Town -Hall, before Mr. J. T. Foard, barrister-at- law, Mr. H. B. Shores (Liberal agent) again referred to the vote of Mr. T. Sutton Timmis, J. P., who was struck off the list on Saturday, and asked his Honour to permit him to recapitulate the circumstances of the case, as possibly his Honour might have overlooked some of the points. He stated that Alderman Gossage, J.P., Mayor of Widnes (whose vote was allowed, as Mr. Madge. Con- servative agent, on public grounds, did not object to ' him), and Mr. Timmis formerly owned and occupied the Widnes Soapworks, trading as William Gossage and Sons. On the 24th May last the firm was transformed into a limited liability company. Since thattime Alder- man Gossage and Mr. Timmis had occupied a private office, No. 115, Waterloo-road, which they rented from the company. It was before a shed in the yard of the works, and was owned and occupied by them. Conse- quently he contended that this constituted successive occupation, and the gentlemen were entitled to burgess votes. The Barrister again ruled that they were not, and as thi, was simply a question of fact he declined to give Mr. Snores permission to appeal against his decision. Mr. Shores then asked for permission to appeal on the ground that the Conservative agent had withdrawn the objection, but his Honour said that the notice was not legal, only being given on the 14th inst., consequently in law there had been no withdrawal,and on that ground also he refused permission to appeal.—Mr. Shores said he had put in a statement as that he could ask the higher court to grant an appeal. All the cases which had been objected to by the assist- ant overseer upon the ground that the claimants had received poor relief, and which upon the application of Mr. Shores had been left over until the evidence of the relieving officer could be obtained, were re-investigated, and in all but one instance the objections failed and the claimants, all of whom were Liberals, were allowed their votes. _ _ _

. —... Out of 542 objections made by the Liberals, over 500 have been sustained, and the total costs they have paid for having frivolously objected amount to half-a-crown.

AT WA.RRINGION.

A rather peculiar incident occurred on Friday morning at the Police Court. As is the custom when the business of the court is over, the Magis- trates' Clerk called out "Any applications to the magistrates ?" As there were none, the court was cleared, and the doors closed, it proved, however, that all the business had not been transact Ad. A well-known tradesman was brought before the magistrates in private and dealt with. Ido not, of course, wish that all the private affairs of everyone should be published to the world, but it is grossly unfair in a court of justice above all places, that fish should be made of one and flesh of another.

No fewer than ten youths were dealt with at the Borough Court on Monday morning for gambling with cards. There can be no doubt that this per- nicious habit is on the increase amongst Warring- ton youths, especially among the poorer classes. The chance of gain tempts them, to risk their coppers freely, and as they usually choose to follow their game oi chance, in the open-air, they very ot ten come under the vigilant eye of the police. The magistrates en Monday seemed determined to check the nabit,and fined one of the youtbs,whom they con- sidered a ringleader, 40s. and costs or in default one month's imprisonment. Whether this will have a salutary effect remains to be seen.

A verdict of" Wilful murder," is of sufficiently rare occurrence near Warrington,to call for remark. At the same time, Mr. Editor, I think that the ver- dict to this effect passed at Grappenhall, at the inquest upon the body of a child which was found in the river Mergey, is to say the least, uncalled for. "Found dead" would have been a much more sensible verdict. Granted that a wire was wound round the body of the child, and that a weight was also attached to it, there was not a particle of evi- dence to prove that the child had ever lived, and therefore the verdict falls to the ground immedi- ately. Possibly, in the present case, the verdict will not make the slightest difference to anybody, for it is very improbable that it will ever be dis- covered who put the body into the river.

Councillor Woods has an especial grievance against the Warrington gas, and he takes every opportunity of endeavouring to prove that it is very bad, aud. totally unfit as an illuminant. He can now congratulate himself upon finding a supporter in a very unexpected quarter, in the person of Mr. J. T. Foatd, the revising barrister. This gentleman was engaged in his duties at the Town Hall from six o'clock to a little atter seven on Monday evening, and, of course, he required the gas. At the close of the sitting, Mr. Hampson made a remark about the gas to which Mr. Foard replied that it was very poor indeed. Mr. Foard cannot be considered to be in any way biassed in the matter,for he is a stranger in the town, and what he said was simply in reply to a casual remark. Councillor Woods will, no doubt, return to the attack with renewed vigour at the earliest possible chance.

The Wesleyans at Bold-street are following in the wake of several other religious bodies in the town, and they are now holding" Sunday afternoon ser- vices for the people." The first meeting was distinctly encouraging, for the body of the large chapel was filled. Provided they continue as they are advertised, namely " bright and brotherly," they should become as great a power in the town as the Wycliffe P.S.A., which I notice has now enrolled over 1,000 members. The Bold-street chapel movement differs in one respect, and I think to its advantage. Ladies as well as gentlemen are allowed to be present at eve ry service, so that the singing especially is a decided improvement upon that in which there are only male voices.

The revision this year has been unusually quiet. In fact with the exception of that relating to Coun- cillor Muir's vote, scarcely any cases were of public interest. The proceedings were very tame indeed. Some amusement was caused by a number of claim- ants asking for their expenses for loss of time in coming to claim their votes. Some of them were quite indignant when the Revising Barrister ex- plained to them that he had no power to allow their expenses except when their votes were objected to. One man in particular, appeared to suffer from a strong sense of injury, and he said that nobody should have his vote. The British constitution will surely fall to pieces after this. This man was not alone in his indigation however, for a member of the fair sex also expressed her intention of claim- ing no more votes. The two political parties do not intend, I understand, to give any list of gains and losses, a course which they have adopted for the past six years. Ido not think that the tables usually given are of the slightest use, for I have noticed in some boroughs that the Liberals and Conservatives have puhlisned lists which com- pletely stultified each other. The agreement of the two parties in Warrington is therefore a very good one.

WARRINGTON MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

Though little more than a month now remains before the annual municipal elections take place, there is very slight interest being taken in them by the Warrington public. Neither Liberals nor Con- servatives have as yet made any move in the direc- tion of securing candidates, other than the retiring councillors for the nine seats which will become vacant on November Ist. The Trades Council appears to be first in the field, as they have selected Mr. James Battersby, the defeated candidate for the Howley, as the Lab Dar candidate for the Whitecross ward. There is, however, plenty of time for fresh developments before the polling day. Another point of interest will also arise this year. Mr. Muir will cease to be a burgess on November Ist, and it is therefore probable that two councillors will be required for the St. Austin's ward, as Coun- cillor J. Fairclough also retires by rotation. The following are the retiring councillors for the various wards this year : Town Hall Ward Whitecross Ward Mr. G. Plumpton. Bewsey Ward Mr. F. Flood. Orford Ward . Mr. J. Baird. St. John's Ward Mr. J. Garner. Fairfield Ward. Howley Ward St. Austin's Ward Mr. J. Fairclough. Latchford TRADES COUNCIL CINDIDATES.

A special meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, was held on Tuesday at the Horse- market-street Cocoa Rooms to take into considera- tion the forthcoming elections both for the Board of Guardians and the Town Council. A discussion arose upon the advisability of contesting one or more of the wards in November next and it was ultimately decided thnt three of the wards be con- tested by candidates selected by the Trades Council; that Mr. T. B. Malkin (president). and Mr. A. Lang be the candidates for the Bewsey ward and St. John's ward respectively for the Board of Guardians, and that Mr. J. Battersby be candidate for the Whitecross ward for the Town Council.

" TOMMY " BURNS BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

" T.4mmy "Burns, the champion diver, was brought before Mr. J. Makinson, the stinendary magistrate at the Salford Policerourt,on Friday morning week, charged with causing an obstruction of the public thoroughfare. Chief Detective-inspector Lyogue said that a public announcement was made that the defendant would dive from Trafford Road Bridge into the Ship Canal about one o'clock on Thursday afternoon. About 1.15 a cab drove up to the bridge followed by a large crowd, who cheered. Inspector Wood stopped the cab, and asked if Burns was in. He was told he was not, and the cab, which was evidently a decoy, was allowed to pass. Im- mediately afterwards defendant drove up in a fish cart, dressed as a fish wife. (Laughter.) He was arrested,and afterwards liberated on bail, thus being able to fill his "engagement "at a music-hall in Manchester. Evidence having been given defendant's manager asked permission to say a few words.—The Stipendary : How do you manage him ? (Laughter.) Manager : I am his business manager, and introduce him on the stage. Witness ex- hibited a shield on which were pinned the medals "Tommy "had had awarded by the Royal Humane Society. He further said the defendant had saved 42 lives, and that he intended to dive from Trafford Road Bridge, not with the idea of creating sensation, but to show that a high dive could be done when it was Inecessary for the purpose of saving life.—Defendant was fined Ws. and costs or 14 days' imprisonment. The fine was paid.
