THE STRANGE DEATH OF A WIDNES

WOMAN.

MAGISTERIAL PROCEEDINGS.

William Allen, aged 45, was brought up at the Widnes Police Court, on Monday, charged on sus- picion with causing the death of his wife. The pre- siding magistrate was Alderman Timmis, Superintendent Keighley said the prisoner was charged on suspicion with causing the death of his wife sotne time between 8 30 on Friday night and 2.15 on Saturday afternoon. The prisoner appeared to have married the deceased woman some two years ago, and they lived very unhappily during that time. About eight months ago he left her, and he made some weekly allowance to her. When the prisoner got home to his lodgings about two o'clock on Saturday afternoon he asked his landlady if his wife had been for her weekly money. The land- lady told prisoner she had not, and he said that before he had his dinner he would take her the usual allowance. He went out to his wife's house,where he found his wife lying on the floor. He then went into a neighbour's ' house and called a woman into the house to see what 1 state his wife was in. The woman went in and saw the I deceased lying on the floor. She took hold of her hand, and finding it cold told the prisoner his wife was dead The prisoner then said she ought to have been dead long. ago. The woman told the prisoner to go for a police- man, and he appeared to have gone to his brother, who I brought in the police. The deceased woman appeared I to have gone home about 9.30 on Friday night, and sent I her little girl, aged eight, who appeared to be a very intelligent child, to the vaults at the corner of Church•street for some beer. The child would only be away a few minutes, and she left her mother sitting on the sofa in the kitchen, but when she came back she found her mother lying on the floor. The little girl then went to an aunt's house in West-street.and asked her aunt to go back with her to Dock-street. The aunt replied that she was getting some supper ready, and cculd not go then. The child wanted to stay the night at this aunt's house, but she was sent to the house of another aunt in the neighbourhood. On her way there she met the prisoner coming from the di- rection of his wife's house in Dock-street, and going in the direction of his lodgings. Therefore, if this was a case of murder or mans aughter it was committed between nine and half-past on Friday night. The prisoner was there (the police court) s)me eight months ago, and had been there several times before,and had threatened what he would do for his wife.

In reply to a question from the bench, Superintendent Keighley said the girl he had referred to had never been home between half-past nine on Friday night until the dead body of her mother was found on the Saturday afternoon, but, strange to say, there was a young child, a boy aged two years, who was in the house all the Fri- day night, and who was noticed playing about on the Saturday morning. No one had seen anything of the deceased after she had returned on the Friday night.

Dr. Hutchinson said that he was called to the house 90, Dock-street, at two minutes to three on Saturday afternoon. He examined the deceased externally, and found a number of bruises on her. She was bruised about the arms and body, and also about the head,while one of her ribs on the right side was fractured. There was an abrasion two inches long under the skin. There were bruises on the left cheek and on the right breast. Some ashes were discovered on the thumb and back of the right hand. Witness could not at present say de- finitely what was the cause of death, but his opinion was that it had been caused by'violence. The young daughter of the deceased, Dorothy, was then called, but not sworn. She said the prisoner was her stepfather. She saw her mother in the house on Friday night at nearly half past nine. She sent witness for a gill of beer to the vaults. Witness would not be away more than five minutes. When she went out her mother was sitting on the sofa, but when she returned her mother was lying on the floor. Witness got hold of her mother's hand, and found that it was cold. She then went to her aunt's house in West-street. She did not tell her aunt about her mother lying on the floor,but asked her if she would come to her mother's house. Her aunt was getting some supper ready. The witness here became very nervouseand the magistrate said bethought sufficient evidence had been given to justify a re- mand.

Superintendent Keighley said he wanted to make fur- ther inquiries, and asked that the prisoner be remanded to the cells instead of Walton Jail until Thursday next. This application was granted. INQUEST AND VERDICT. Mr. Brighouse on Tuesday, opened an inquest at Wid- nes on the body of Jane Allen, of 90, Dock-street, who was found dead on Saturday. William Allen, labourer, the woman's husband, was on Monday remanded at Widnes Police Court on suspicion of causing her death, and was present in custody. Mr. J. W. Knowles, solici- tor. appeared for Allen. Evidence was given to the effect that Allen and his wife had separated owing to the latter's indulgence in drink. Dorothy Morris,aged eight years, daughter of the woman, said she told no one on the Friday or S tturday about finding her mother on the floor on returning from the publichouse. Her little brether two years old, was sitting on the sofa when she went out and when she returned. Superintendent Keighley said the child must have been there all night, as it had picked rind eaten the middle out of a loaf. Dr. Hutchinson said he and Dr. O'Keefe made a post-mortem examination on Monday. The cause of death was viol- ence. The violence to the head and chest was sufficient to cause death. A fall could not have caused all the injuries. Thomas Allen, prisoner's nephew, stated that he was with the accused at the Main Top public-house from eight o'clock until nine, and evidence was also given that the prisoner returned to his lodging at half - past nine o'clock perfectly sober. The Coroner said that unless there was some further evidence to connect the prisoner with the death than they had at present, even if the woman died from the injuries received at some one's hand, they could not send the man for trial on the present evidence. It would be unfair. After further police evidence, the prisoner, William Allen, having been cautioned by the coroner,was called by Mr. Knowles, and after being sworn, he gave account of his movements en the Friday night. He was in the Main Top public-house until nine o'clock, and then he took a friend home. It was after this he was seen in Oates- street. He went straight home, had his supper,and went to bed at ten o'clock. He was not at his wife's house at all on the Friday night, nor did he see her that day. The hist time he saw his wife alive was on Thursday. The Coroner, in summing up, said he did not think the jury could come to any other conclusion than that the woman died from violence, but he could net see how they could from the evidence say that the violence was caused by the prisoner. Their proper verdict he thought was that the woman died from violence, but how that was caused there was no evidence to show. The jury, after an absence of seven minutes' duration, returned an open verdict. After the inquest the prisoner was taken before a magistrate and discharged.

RATS FEASTING ON MUSIC.

At the Marylebone County Court on Monday, before Judge Stoner and a jury, the case was beard of Willis v. Norris, which was a claim for damage alleged to have be done to plaintiff's geods by rats while being ware- housed at the defendant's repository at Harrow-road. The plaintiff is the mother of Miss Blanche Wolseley, a member of Mr. D'Ovly Carte's Opera Company, and in examination by Mr. Colain stated that in 1890 her daughter entered upon a touring engagment in the north of England, in consequence of which the witness, who was to accompany her, decided to warehouse certain household goods with the defentant. Amongst them was a packing case full of opera and other music, which was covered and securely bound with cord. Miss Wolseley's theatrical engagement did not terminate until February this year, and during the interval the plaintiff paid £2O for warehousing the goads. The goods were sent home, and on the following afternoon Miss Wolseley and some friends commenced to unpack the goods in order to find the music of" The Bohemian Girl." While the unpack- ing was proceeding six large rat, to the horror of the ladies present, jumped out. The witness dragged the packing case into the garden and a young man jumped over the wall and killed the rats with a stick. When the packing case was further examined it was discovered that " The Bohemian Girl " and a quantity of other music had been devoured by the rats, while other pro- perty had been destroyed. The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff, damages £lO, and the learned judge gave costs on the higher scale.

A new carriage, built for the German Emperor, is provided with electric lamps,which contain some accumulators. One is placed on the breast of each horse, also on the point of the pole of the carriage, at the sides, and at the rear of the vehicle. Thus distributed, they will make the route at night as light as at noonday for a distance of about fifty paces from the equipage, while the vehicle itself with its Imperial occupants, will remain in perfect darkness.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 22, 1894.

THE BISHOP Of CHESTF:It iti, as Public-House most people now know, a strong Reform.

advocate of the Gothenburg system of conducting the liquor traffic. That is to say, he is in favour of the nationalisation of the liquor trade. If his plan were adopted all the public- houses of the country would be practically placed under the direct control of the State authorities, or, more probably, under the control of the local authority of each district. The Bishop has en- bated the sympathy and support of some influen- tial men, including Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, and, most influential of all, Mr. GLADSTONE. The letter from Mr. GLADSTONE which has been published this week for the first time is, undoubtedly, an important pronouncement, and it has been re- ceived as such throughout the country. The late Premier declares that "for many years I have been strongly of opinion the principle of selling liquors for the public profit only oflered the sole chance of escape from the present miserable and almost contemptible predicament whiclk is a disgrace to the country." He is friendly to local option, but he considers that "it can be no more than a partial and occasional remedy." The mere limitation of the number of public-houses is,he considers,of little use; in fact as a remedy for the present evils it is simply an "imposture." All schools of temperance reformers, and most other people besides, will cordially agree with him when he says that the growth of the system of tied houses continually aggravates the prevailing mischief. And in this connection it is satisfactory to note that the Crewe magistrates, who have taken a particularly strong stand against the custom of putting "remov- able" managers into public-houses, have not been intimidated or disheartened by the action of the Cheshire Quarter Sessions in reversing their decision of last year. They have again this year given Messrs. PETER WALKER and SONS, Limited, the choice of altering the tenancy of their houses or of having their licenses refused. The brewing firm have chosen— so it is reported—to make their managers into tenants, and although the changes in tenancy, have not gone quite far enough to satisfy the Crewe Bench tee licenses have been granted this

year. All this indii:at s that there is an irresisti- ble tendency in the direction of public-house re- form. What shape that reform will ultimately take it is as yet impossible to predict. But one thing is clear beyond cavil, and it is this—the pre- sent system of conducting the liquor traffic is doomed. It is now, as a whole, practically with- out its defenders. The real problem of the future is to find out the way by which the system can best be reformed—or abolished. With regard to Mr. GLADSTONE'S letter there is little doubt that the opponents of local option will attempt to make capital out of it. But as a matter of fact, there is no necessary antago- nism between local option and the Gothenburg system. If the Bishop of Chester's scheme were adopted tomorrow there would still be the same demand on the ground of justice that particular localities should have right of deciding whether or not the traffic should be carried on in any shaPe or form. On the other hand, the passing of the Local Option Bill into law would be no maser' for the adherents of the Gothenburg system abandon- ing their agitation. Even the most ardent local optionist is not so sanguine as to believe that the passing of Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT'S bill will bring about a total extinction of the drink trade. That trade would still be carried on in but too nianY localities, and there would still be ample scope for the reforming zeal of the Gothenburger.

WE printed last week a letter, Licensing signed by most of the Noncon- Matters. formist ministers of Warrington as well as by other townsmen, in which approval was expressed of the action of the Rev. D.

ROBERTS in connection with the recent Brewster Sessions. This was a public tribute which was due to Mr. ROBERTS, and as a matter of fact the letter

will be endorsed by a large number of VVarring-

_ tonians who have the best interests of the town

at heart. The course taken by Mr. ROBERTS WaSY in view of all the circumstances, courageous and . self-sacrificing in a high degree, and would 114 - have been undertaken by anyone who was not act"

ing under a strong sense of public duty. The un- scrupulous and ignorant abuse which has been freely lavished upon Mr. ROBERTS was one of tile inevitable penalties, and was no doubt freely dis- counted beforehand. As a matter of fact, so long as there is the present distrust in regard to the manner in which the licensing laws are carried, out so long will public-spirited men feel„hulDelle". to force the hands of, and, so to speak, set all example to, those in authority. At the Brewsttr Sessions the allegation was made, and never cen" tradicted, that the police are under the necessitY of reporting all cases against publicans to the Watch Committee before the magistrates ever hear of them, and of taking the instructions of the committee. This being so, is the prevailing dis" trust to be wandered at? The members of the Watch Committee certainly ought, on their own account, to explain what part they take in regard to these cases. Is the making of the report bYd the police merely a formal and usual incident, all. 1) are the cases passed on as a matter of course • the magistrates? Or, are the cases privately judicated upon, and either sent before or keP from the magistrates as it may please the C°13:1; mittee ? These are very important questions materially affect the administration of justiee 111 the borough.

A RUNCORN PUBLICAN FINED AT BLACKPOOL. A disagreeable sequel to the recent encampment ot the Runcorn Volunteers at Blackpool has just coma to light. George Shone,landlord of the George and Dragon Hotel, Egerton-street, Runcorn, was charged on Wednes- day at Kirkham Police Court with selling drink without a license in a canteen of the 2nd V.B.C.R. at Layton, Blackpool on the stn August. It was stated by the pro- secution that at 10 50 a.m. on the day mentioned two constables were watching the canteen and saw eight civilians enter it. The o:ficers went in and saw three civilians drinking. P.C. Haynes ordered two glasses of beer, which were served. Defendant, who was at the back of the canteen was catering for the 2nd Cheshire Volunteers. Be said, when questioned, that he had a magistrate's six days' license which he produced. The license bore the words "Sunday omitted." Orders had been given to supply no civilians and the policemen came into the canteen with some volunteers.—Defendant was fined 20s. and costs. A" BRUMMAGEM BOXER" IN TROUBLE AT RUNCORN. At the Runcorn Police Court before Messrs. Philip Speakman, C.C., and Samuel Taylor, William Maybury was charged with being drunk and disorderly on the previous day, in Church-street.—P.C. Gregory testified to seeing the defendant in Church-St. in an intoxicated state. The officer saw him go into a small shop, take a pound of "black puddings" from the counter and put them in his pocket. When spoken to by the witness (who at the time was wearing plain clothes) defendant, with an oath, wanted to know what the matter had to do with him. He further said that he (defendant) was "a boxer from Brummagem," and that witness had better make himself scarce or there would be something in store for him. These dire threats had no elect how- ever on the doughty representative of the law and he forthwith attempted to take " Brummagem Bill" to the lock-up. The latter then became extremely violent un- til the officer was forced to seek the assistance of other constables to take him to the police station. The woman whose black puddings defendant had taken, declined to prosecute for that offence.—ln ans wer to the Bench defendant said he had come from BirMiEg ham to repair some sewing machines at Widnes.—Fined 53. and costs or seven days' imprisonment. DEATH OF Mn. GEORGE WILLIAMS OF RUNCORN. An old figure in Runcorn life has just passed away in the person of Mr. George Williams, 68 years of age, and lately a resident of Ashridge-street. Mr. Williams came to Runcorn more than forty years ago, an that he was regarded by many persons as a native of the town. For a long term of years he had been in the employ of the Bridgewater Canal Undertaking at Runcorn, and by his genial disposition had made a host of friends. He was a member of the Shepherds' Club, Runcorn, for many years, and was connected with it at the time of his decease. During the past twelve months he had been in failing health, and almost incessantly, Dr. McKenzie, his medical adviser, had been in attendance upon him. On Sunday morning he was sal zed with an attack of apoplexy,from which he never rallied,but sank and expired on Tuesday morning. The remains were interred on Thursday afternoon in Runcorn Cemetery, the Rev. H. B. Firth impressively reading the burial service. The following were among the mourners : First coach : Mr. George Williams (son), Mr. George Shone, Sergeant Harvey, and Sergeant Holmes (sons-in- law), and Mr. T. G. Shone (grandson) ; second coach : Dr. McKenzie, Captain Trimble, and Mr. Robert Hand- ley. A number of choice floral wreaths were placed upon the coffin. PICNIC TO DELAMERE FOREST FROM RUNCORN. The annual picnic of teachers -and elder scholars con- nected with the Bethesda Congregational Church, Run- corn, teok place on Wednesday when a company, num- bering about 30, were conveyed to the forest of Dela- mere in saloon buses belonging to Messrs Yarwood and Son. A start was made shortly after ten o'clock, the ride to Eddisbury Lodge ,being much enjoyed. The atmos- pheric conditions were not of the fairest,but the pleasure was not marred thereby in the least as every member of the party used the utmost endea- vour to make the best of the circumstances. On alighting from the conveyances at the Lodge some of the picnicers repaired to the forest where they delighted themselves by taking short walks among the stout old trees, and viewing nature in her approach- ing autumn garb. The more gamesome spirits of the party organized various sports in which they indulged with a great deal of zest. The requirements of the inner man were supplied by Misses Riley and Robb, who prepared a very tasty tea, which was partaken of with the keenest relish. At length the return journey was undertaken and finished in good time. A stop was made at Frodsham for a short interval to allow the party to wit- ness the parade of cyclists through the village. The Rev. John Simpson, pastor of Bethesda Church; and Mr. W. H. Chadwick, superintendent of the Sunday school.were among the excursionists. ANOTHER CRUELTY CASE AT WIDNES. On Thursday at the Widnes Petty Sessions, before the Mayor (Alderman F. H. Gossage) and Mr. H. Wade Deacon,John Grady and his wife Kate Grady,of Caroline- street, were summoned by the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children for neglecting their five children—Mary Ann, ten years old, John, nine, Elizabeth, six, Winifred, three, and Patrick, three months—on divers dates between the Bth of May and the Bth inst. in such a manner as to cause them unnecessary pain and suffering. Mr.J. W. Knowles prosecuted. The male defendant did not appear, and the case was heard in his absence. Inspector McAteer deposed to visiting the house of the defendants, 28, Caroline-st. He found the female defendant and her five children in the back kitchen. The children were in a most deplorable state, They were very poorly clad, and there was very little furniture in the house. Two old mattresses he found upstairs, but there was no furniture. The house was in a most filthy state, and not fit for human habitation. There was no food whatever in the house. Witness asked the female defendant how she could account for the filthy state of the house and her children, and she said she could not say. She further stated that her husband was in ve)rk and earned 193. a week. He gave her 15s. a week. and sometimes more. Afterwards she said her husband drank his earnings, and got drunk two or three days a week. Witness visited the house again on the 11th and 14th inst., but no one was in. and he did not gain admission.—Dr. O'Keefe, who accompanied the Inspector, corroborated this evidence. Inspector Strick- land said 'he had knowa the family for the last twenty years. The man was a 1 azy, drunken man, who would not work, and his wife had habitually neglected her family.—The bench sent the woman to gaol for fourteen days with hard labour, and ordered a warrant for the apprehension of the husband. A WIDNES PUBLICAN FINED. On Thursday, at the Widnes Petty Sessions, before the Mayor (Alderman F. H. Gossage), and Mr. H. Wade Deacon, Thomas Whimpenny, landlord of the Moss Bank Hotel, Moss Bank, Widnes, was summoned by the police I for selling drink during prohibited hours on the 3rd inst. I Mr. J. 0. Swift, St Helens, defended. Four men ,named Charles Riley, Elizabeth-st. ; John Thomas, Farrant-st. ; Edward Fallon, Victoria-St. ; and Patrick Lackey, Vic- toria-st. ; were charged with being on licensed premises during prohibited hours—namely, nine minutes before six in the morning. Police-sergeant M'Gregor stated that from information received about half-pest five on the morning of the 3rd of the present month he went in plain clothes, in company with Police-constable Hamil- ton, to the defendant's heuse,and concealed himself near I the premises. At that time the doors of the house were all closed. At eleven minutes to six o'clock, however, he saw the vaults door of the defendant's house open,and a man went in, and came out a mifiute afterwares wip- ing his mouth. Four other men afterwards went in,and witness followed. The defendant and his son were in the bar, and the defendant was filling a glass with what appeared to be stout, for which John Thomas paid hies. The other three men were standing at the counter, and each had before him a pint glass, the contents of which had been partly drunk. The clock in defendant's bar showed the time as exactly six o'clock, but the correct time was nine minutes to six c'clock. When witness served the summons on the 18th inst., Whimpenny said, "I know it was a few minutes to the time, but it is no- thing serious ; it was a mistake."—Police-constable Hamilton corroborated.—Mr. Swift, for the defence, said it was a mistake on the part of the defendant, who was heartily sorry it had occurred.—The bench imposed a fine of 20s. and costs.—The charge against the four men was dismissed.

CULCHETH CHAPEL SERMONS. The anniversary sermons in connection with the Independent Methodist Chapel, Culcheth, were preached by Mr. H, E. Roscoller, of Warrington, on Sunday. There were good congregations at both afternoon and evening services. The choir rendered special hymns and anthems in an efficient manner. A collection in aid of the trust fund realised a good sum.

Mr. 'Hutt, who has charge of the mission, was unable to I be present, but sent an apology. '

= THE EXAMINER SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 22, 1894.

DISTRICT COUNCILS.

TOWN'S MEETING AT EARLESTOWN. A largely attended meeting of the ratepayers and householders was held in the Town Hull, Earles- town, on Thursday evening, for the purpose of considering the question of the forthcoming elections in connection with the new District Council. Mr. S. Harvey opened the meeting by moving that Mr. J. W. Emmett be asked to preside over the meeting. He had, he said attended there not at all in the capacity of a Commissioner, but as a townsman like the rest of them. Mr. Emmett moved an amendment that Mr. Harvey preside. The first motion was seconded, and unanimous- ly carried, and Mr. Emmett thereupon took the chair, the only other occupant of the platform being Mr. Harvey. The Chairman said he was at a loss, to a very great extent to know how to proceed with the meeting. Be did not for one moment expect to be called upon as chairman. As Mr. Harvey had already explained he came there as a listener and not as a speaker in any shape ox form, and he was sorry Mr. Harvey did I not put his amendment in place of the motion, for he thought Mr. Harvey was the right person to have I presided over the meeting. He really did not know it had been called, nor who had called it. He took it the main object of the meeting was in order to select candidates for the new council which would be elected some time in December, he believed. What they had to do that night was to frame some scheme as to how best to get candidates for thediew council. They were all aware that the township had been divided into five wards, and each ward would have to be represented by three councillors,and in order to '.et these three coun- cillors in each ward, it had suggested itself to him that it would be best to form a committee for each ward,say a committee of six, with, of course, power to add to their number, in order that they might receive nomina- tions for candidates. After the whole of the committees had been elected, then he should say a central committee ought to be formed, and the sub., or executive commit- tees report to that central committee what they had done, to bring names before it of those who had been nominated in order that they might select from the num. bet nominated the best candidates for the position. (Hear, hear.) That was just an outlaw of what he thought .should be done. If any gentleman had any suggestions to make he should be pleased to hear, and answer them to the best of his ability, and then they could get on with the business. It was for them and himself,as rate- payers,to do the best they could and select the best men tney could, in order to, represent the ratepayers on the District Council. (Applause.) Mr. S. Harvey observed that the Chairman had said he knew very little about the origin of the meeting. Perhaps it would be as well if he threw some light on it. They knew there had been a common feeling of antici- pation through the town,that something should be done, and must be done, in view of the approaching elections. But there was no one officially to move in the vote. If I anything was said to the Commissioners they said they would be the last persons to take any steps, because it might be said they were fishing for public confidence to retain their their seats. So it fell out,that when there was no one to act officially, it rested with somebody to act unefficially. He ha e not aoted at all singly in the matter, but different peiple had spoken to him about the matter, and had said, other folks were i moving, and the townspeople should be mov- ing there, for a selection of candidates. He at last went to Mr. Molyneux, who suggested that meeting. They got the distinct consent of some 20 or 30 gentlemen and upon them rested the responsibility of calling that meeting and of bearing what little expenses were con- nected with it. The new Act of Parliament brought to them such changes in the government of the town, that it was right they as townsmen should have some idea about them. There was the matter of dividing the town- ship into wards, but it might be that everyone was not conversant with the laying out of those wards, He then proceeded to give in detail the boundaries of the various wards. The division into 5 wards necessitated an in- crease of members from 10 to 15, and there were certain provisions in the act which would make the newly con- stituted body of much greater importance than the pre- sent Commissioners were. The chairman of that body would have the honour of beinga county magistrate for the time being. Then the Council had some increased powers, somewhat dependent upon the County Council, but of great importance there just now with regard to common rights, side wastes and that sort of thing. (Applause.)—On their present Board there were 17 members resident in Earlestown, viz.. Messrs. Emmett, Ormand, Evans, McMahon, Thompson, Ratcliffe and Forster. Colonel McCorquodale and Mr. Smith were Newton residents, and Mr. Coiling- wood was a Wirgrave resident. Nine of those gentle- men were certainly eligible, whether they were disposed or not, to be re-elected. But supposing they were all returned again, there would be six new members to be elected from somewhere, so that they would see the im- portance of making a wise selecticn of candidates. With- out some public feeling or public action they might have 20 or 30 candidates springing up all over the place, some of them with a remote chance of success. There- fore they were there that night and were entirely in the hands of the meeting to say what should be done. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman said it must be understood that they were leaving Newton out of the question altogether. They were making their own arrangements. He thought the best plan would be for that meeting to form Ward Committees and then let those committees receive nominations, and if there were more candidates in the field than were required, then it would be for them to weed them down and select the best and strongest men. Mr. Pryor then moved and Mr. R. Whitfield seconded, "That a Joint Committee be formed from that meeting, of six representatives for each ward, and that that com- mittee be empowered to appoint district Ward Com- mittees to receive nominations, to consider them, and to report back to the General Committee."—The mot ion was unanimougly carried. The Chairman thought it should be understood that whatever condidates came forward, their names should be brought before another ratepayers' meeting in order that their selection might be ratified. The folloaing Committees were then chosen : Town Hall Ward :—Messrs. J. Ashcroft, D. Forrester, J. Stringer, T. Scott, E. Bunting and Rev. T. R. Matthews. Viaduct Ward :—Dr. Valentine, Dr. Mouncey, Messrs. T. Molyneux, G. Crossley, W. Gilmour and S. Harvey. Crow-lane Ward :—Messrs. W. Davies, Schofield, T. Ormond, T. Latimer, G. Barrett, and Rev. E. W. W. Kaye Wargrave Ward :—Messrs. Haselden, Slee, Pryor, Ralson, Rodgers and Wright. After a vote of thanks to the Chairman the meetio g dispersed.

DISTRESSING SUICIDE AT RUNCORN.

A BLACKSMITH DROWNS HIMSELF IN THE

SHIP CANAL. A distressing case of selt-destruction occurred at Runcorn on Thursday morning,just after six o'clock, the victim b. ing a blacksmith named Frederick Williams, who had resided until Wednesday at Dunham-&-th'-llill. He was about 31 years of age, and for a considerable time has been of unsound mind. On Wednesday he left his home at Dunham-o'-th-Hill,accom- panied by his sister, and journeved to Runcorn,whence he had come to visit his brother Tnomas,at N 0.9, Hartley- st. His sister left him with Thomas,and returned home. They retired to l-ed in the usual way, and all went well until 6.15 the following morning, when Frederick jumped up from his bed, partially 'dressed himself, and left the bedroom saying to his brother Thomas that he was going to the convenience in the yard. The suspicions of Thomas seemed to be aroused,however,and he,l!sten- ing, heard his brother open the yard gate. He imme- diately got up and followed as fast as he could. He saw his brother running down Irwell-lane and just as he reached the bottom he got on to the Ship Canal bank and jumped into the water. A flatman named Wm. Til- lman, of Weston Point, who was near the Old Quay, also saw the occurrence, and procured a boat hook with the intention of rendering the drowning man some assistance. The hook was too short, however, to reach him, and he flung out a rope, but Williams did not take it. A life-buoy was then brought by Guy Pearce, lock master, and it was thrown to the man. This al§o Williams did not apparently try to grasp, and he there- upon sank. Grappling operations at once began, and it was not until 9.45 that the body of the unfortunate man was recovered from the water about one hundred yards '.'rom the spot where he jumped in. It was conveyed to 9, Hartley-street.—The Coroner has been informed of the circumstances, and it is expected that be will hold an inquiry this (Friday) afternoon, or Saturday morn- ing.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR.

-- The Presbyterians of Runcorn are assidiously working for two laudable objects, firstly to liquid- ' ate the debt remaining on the new school, class- rooms, and the land they have recPntly acquired for the purpose of erecting a church thereon; and secondly to raise sufficient funds to proceed with the building of the church. It is well-known, by this time, to everyone, that they have removed from their old place of worship, and have for some time past, been worshipping in the new school off Vic- toria-road. By great efforts they at last succeeded in placing themselves in a position to justify them in building this new school, and now they are earnestly appealing to all their Christian friends to assist them in accumulating the required capital to begin the erection of the long-looked-for church on the plot of land in Victoria-road. Although the school referred to serves admirably the purposes for which it was originally intended, and has been a worshipping place for the congregation since they removed from the old church, yet there is obvicusly not the convenience and accommodation which is requisite for devotional purposes. One of the means by which the Committee hope to realise the required money is the organizing of a bazaar, which they inzend to hold on three successive days, beginning on the 3rd October.

It is a matter for grave regret,if rumours are tobe trusted, that parents should be guilty of so much carelessness as to knowingly alow their children to wander hbout the streets and play with other chil- dren when suff,ring from any infectious disease. Only at the last meeting of the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority, when the medical officer (Dr. Adams) re- ported several cases of fever, a member remarked that he had been told, and knew it to be a fact that certain parents had allowed their childrer to go to school whilst troubled with au infectious disease. The Medical Officer also stated that he had heard of a similar case himself, in the course of his inquiries. lie referred to the urgent necessity for an isolation hospital for such diseases and to the difficulty which existed in successfully coping with some cases. The committee who have in hand the provision of a hospital are giving a great (teal of attention to the matter, and hope soon to make a statement of their position. It is, however, of extreme importance that parents should evercise the utmost vigilance over their children when affected with any infect- ious complaint and so prevent them from going into the streets or schools and mixing with their play- mates.

The report presented by Mr. Jones, librarian of the Runcorn Free Library,at the last monthly meet- ing of the Committee on the proceedings of the Con- ference of the Libraries' Association at Belfast, whither he had been sent to represent the com- mittee, proved to be of great interest. Informa- tion which Mr. Jones at some future time may use for the benefit of library habitues, was given on several subjects at the Conference, some of it being of great importance. Although the Committee's action in incurring the expense without the sanetion of the Board of Commissioners may be con- sidered an irregularity, yet it cannot be truthfully stated that the journey was a fruitless one. It was unfortunate that the Committee had not sufficient time to bring the matter in a straightforward manner before the Commissioners, for then most likely nothing would have been heard of it. How- ever, the Board's protest will serve as a warning for the future guidance of the Committee.

The Commissioners are apparently moving effec- tively in the question of re-establtshing communi- cation between Runcorn and Widnes by means of the old ferry. Considerable correspondence has taken place between them and the parties concerned in the matter,and this week I heard that Sir Leader Williams had visited Runcorn and had had a con- ference with our surveyor. It has not yet been made know what the result of the interview was, but in some reliable quarters it is believed that the time is not far off when the shores of Runcorn and Widnes will once more be linked together as in the good old days of yore. A large number of persons will undoubtedly be greatly convenienced by the re-opening of the ferry. Especially will this be so with the poorer class of folk who—when fate makes it imperative that they should remove themselves and their chattels to that salubrious locality—will not be put to the expense of carting their furniture around by Warrington or making numberless journeys across the bridge in order to shuffle out of our presence. Oh I may that day not be far off.

MINERS AND THEIR WAGES.

TEST CASE AT ST. HELENS. At St. Helens County Court on Wednesday,before Judge Collier, William Birch, collier, of Crank-lane, Windle, sued Messrs. Glover, Urmson, and Glover, proprietors of the Phcenix Colliery, for £2 2s.,wages alleged to be due in lieu of a week's notice. Mr. J. P. Mearns appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. J. 0. Swift for the firm.—Mr. Mearns, in stating the plaintiff's case, re• marked that the action was taken to test tho powers of the proprietors of that and other collieries to do as they liked with their men. At the various collieries in the district the proprietors fixed what was called the " mak- ing-up day "—that was a day for making up the wages due to each man, and the wages were paid a fixed num- ber of days afterwards. The proprietars seemed to think that at their discretion they could alter both the pay day and the " making-up day" to suit their own convenience. At the Phcenix Colliery it was usual to make-up on Tuesday, and pay on Friday. On the 11th May last, Mr. Marsh, the manager, served Birch with seven days' notice. This notice, however, was served on a Friday, and he (Mr. Mearns) contended that that was a bad notice, as it ought to have been served on Tuesday. The" making-up day" was almost without variation, the day on which notices were given. He therefore sued for one week's wages in lieu of proper notice. Other men got notice on the same day, but they were taken back, and Mr. Mearns contended that the exception was made in Birch's case because a fine imposed upon him by Marsh had been given back to him by the employers.—The plaintiff, Mr. John Mather (cheekweighman at the Phcenix Colliery), Mr. George Dixon (checkweighman at Ashton's Green Col)iery),and Mr. T. Glover, miners' agent, were then called to give evidence that the custon4 in the mining industry was for all notices to be given on " making-up days.' In cross-examinatien by Mr. Swift, the witnesses admitted that there were no printed rules to that effect.—Before the defence was entered upon, his Honour said he did think it was material whether it was the custom to give notice on the" making-up day," because in that case the plaintiff accepted the notice given to him and acted upon it. Ile thought, however, that it would be much more convenient to have a clear understanding between employers and employed that notice should only be given on "making-UP day." He allowed Mr. Mearns to withdraw the case, and made no order as to costs.

NOTES FROM LIVERPOOL AND DISTRICT.

(BY A LIMB OF THE LAW.)

There has been quite an animated discussion in the city,over the course persued by the Lord Mayor in having called upon the Rev, R. A. Armstrong (who is a dissenter) to say "grace" at the luncheon to the Duke and Duchess of York.—Not to have called upon the Bishop of Liverpool has not only be looked upon by some as an" insult" but a" sacrt- lege"as well. It is singular that in this supposed en- lightened 19th century churchmen will insist upon their so-called "rights" and think that in this case the Lord Mayor has acted with bad taste, &c. -They do not appear to consider that it is possible that it has been bad taste on their part to take advantage of their so-called privileges for so many centuries and that it would be an act of grace at least to give the dissenters such a small privilege as one of their number saying "grace "! However it is only a "storm in a teapot" as the Lord Mayor was acting within his legal rights.

I observe that it is intended if possible to form a cotton market in Manchester and several Liverpool brokers have been ,asked to" assist." The brokers. here at present however think it would be disad- vantageous—of course they mean for themselves. However, no doubt the Ship Canal managers will go on persevering and accomplish their aim in time, when no doubt the Liverpool brokers will then see how advantageous it is to their own interests to " assist." Oh yes the pocket has a wonderful influence over a man's opinions.

That is a fine piece of work that was done by the Dallam Forge Co. at the Landing Stage. It must have found work tor a good many weeks at that establishment. The large" embayment " has been filled in by the pontoons and girders made there, and now the stage is practically quite straight, and is a finer promenade than ever. It has been chris- tened the "Lungs of Liverpool," and no doubt the breezes to be obtained there are tine for the human lungs. The other day I saw an incident there—and by the way, incidents are quite common on the Landing Stage. A poor Englishwoman emigrant bound with her little ones, was on her way to America, and through some oversight one of the little children had strayed away aped was lost. The poor woman's grief seemed uncontrollable, awl tile child was missing for hours.

Who is supposed to look after the graveyard at Hill Cliff? Not only do the graves of the departed appear to be neglected, but there is a great look of disorder over the whole place. The yard is mot beautifully situated and a little money judiciously spent in trimming and gardening would not only make a considerable change in its appearance but would repay itself in the enhanced value of the graves. I ha?pened to be in the vicinity of Hill Cliff and paid a visit to the place, and regretted to see this historical yard in its present condition,

The subject of the decline of ship building on the Mersey is again to the fore, and this time the Mer- sey Docks and Harbour Board are being blamed. The " Engineer " states that the Board charges too much rent for its yards. The prevailing opinion in Liverpool for a long time has been that the de- cline was caused through disputes between masters and men over the rate of wages, and that most of the trade gradually went to the Clyde. This re minds me that Warrington at one time did a little shipbuilding, and I have often heard some of the old Warringtonians speak with pride of the " Tay- leur " which was unfortunately wrecked. Now that .the Ship Canal is opened is it possible that Warrington may again see some ship building?

HOW TO BE UNMARRIED THOUGH

lIAPP Y.

(ALL WRONGS DESERVED). While gloat the shattered ghosts of tingling bells, And cow-frogs cower down the brazen dells, The pensive lions sigh in mail-clad glee, And leave the universe to crowds and me. Hard by the crested reeds and stalwart mud, The tar-and-feathered mansion meekly stood ; The foaming petronels propped up the sky. And poured paint-pots in piles for maids who dye! In vain the cyclist pumps his busted tyre, As frosted mutton climbs the curfew spire ; In vain the bloated pauper airs his pride, In vain the lurchers lurch—in vain they slide. Hark! hear the Grand Old (Ger)man band so gay, " Home, sweet Home-Rule Britannia "they play— See Irish Bills sent to the Lords on high, Who chuck them out, and wink the other eye ! Athwart the calamine and glistening sewer, Note what our Deputationists endure; They mix the Dingle draught with mountain dew, And spiral-wise their devious ways pursue! The sighing lover hugs his hoards of gold, While melting maids as modestly grow old; See martial bands in pomp parade the street, Proclaiming " Blackham's Blacking can't be beat !" Perennially our pigments shed their w )01, And fast our spirit flaskets grow unfull ! Although " 'tis neer too late to have a npree," The purblind parokeet sides not with me. Along our streets the wheel-man bumps his way, O'er time-worn flints in tsirturous array ; A spin o'er circler saws, or a new-ploughed field, Would, in comparison, "smooth sailing" yield! sporadic vampires grace the lusty morn, Phonetic flashes fleck the strident corn, Plethoric hag-pipes pave the Milky-way, While all the time bald hairs keep turning grey. Then beat the banjo, chime the piping drum ! Let no man's pride denote his heart is dumb ; Spurn not the rampant strength of sundered vows, But daily quail beneath your babbling spouse ! ADAM NEWSENSE.

Wartitugton, September, 1894. (To be perpetuated).

SHIP CANAL.

--- RUNCORN RURAL SANITARY AUTHORITY AND THE ASSESSMENT OF THE CANAL. The Manchester Ship Canal Company have informed the Assessment Committee of the Runcorn Rural Sani- tary Authority, that they are willing to accept the decision of the Committee to assess the watering at £350 per mile, and that the charges already in force for docks, ctc. shall be enforced.

A REMARKABLE CYCLE RIDE.

On Thursday, T. R. Garton, of the Makerfield Cycling Club made a successful attempt to lower the 100 miles Club record whirh stood to the credit of Mr. T. Cooper at 6 hours and 47 minutes, and at the same time to qualify for the Gold Medal which is given by the Club to those members who ride 100 miles on the road in 7 hours or under. The course selected was the Anfield 50 Miles twice over. The first 50 miles was ridden in 2 hows 36 minutes, which is record for the Club for that distance, the previous best being 2 hours 47 minutes. The full 100 miles was covered in 5 hours 43 minutes (beating the Previous best by 1 hour 4 minutes) which time would have been still considerably reduced had the rider not been most unlucky in he last 50 miles, in which his tyre punctured causin? considerable delay, and he had also to finish the last 10 miles alone owing to the back tyre of a tandem going wrong, and the single machines not being fast enough to pace him. To show the importance of this ride it might be stated that up to some two months ago the Northern Record for the 100 miles stood at 5 hours 45 minutes. The machine he rode was a Rover Road Racer, 26Ibs weight, with Dunlop Special Road Racing Tyres, on which he has this season won the Inter-Club (Makerfield and Golborne) 50 Miles Championship,and also the 50 Miles Inter-Club Handicap from scratch. With good weather it is expected he will further reduce the above time for the 50 miles before the end of the season.

BELSBY.

FANCY DRESS CYCLE PARADE.—With the commenda- ble object of augmenting the funds at the disposal of the managers for the erection of a porch to the Helsby National Schools a grand fancy dress cycle parade took place on Wednesday evening at Helsbv. The Bev. T. W. Vale, vicar of Flelsby ; Messrs. Bowyer, McWaters (joint secretaries). Hill, Sucilow, Bibby, Coppock (Ches- ter), and Collier acted as a committee and were respon- sible for all arrangements. Not,ithstanding the threatening aspect of the weather tlie,e was a capital turnout of cyclists from the clubs of Runcorn,Frodsham, Helsby, Chester. and the surrounding district. Shortly before eight o'clock the procession started from the Britannia Telegraph Works,the lead being taken by two wheelmen with their machines ingeniously rigged with Japanese lanterns. Following them were the members of the Flelsby Church Brass Band, conducted by Mr. Bate. Then came the lielsby Fire Brigade fully equipped, accompanied by the eneine drawn by two horses, and superintended by Mr. Barlow. The rear was brought up by about eighty cyclists with iron steeds of the most ancient and modern types. HOW TO ELECT THE PARISH

COUNCIL.

The Local Government Board have issued the rules for the election of parish councillors, and a copy has been sent to the overseers of every rural parish in England and Wales containing a popula- tion of 300 and upwards, to every County Council, and to the clerk of the guardians of every Poor-law Union which contains parishes entitled to a council of their own.

===== 0002601_18940922_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PARISH MEETING.

The parish meeting -1,-;r:—th-e—fi--;s-t—iilection of parish councillors is to be held on Tuesday, December 4th, not earlier than six o'clock. This will be the first and pos- sibly the only stage of the proceedings, so that every man and woman with a vote will do well to be present. Public notice of the time and place of meeting will be given by the overseers not later than November 24th. The nomination pap‘-rs, copies of which may be had from the overseers beforehand,are to be handed in to the chairman of the parish meeting, who will state to the meeting the names and addresses of the candidates whose nomination he declares to be valid. Alter this statement opportunity will be given for the candidates to be questiJned and to make any explanations. Having thus cleared the ground, and allowed time for the with- drawal of candidates who do not intend to stand, the chairman will have to see whether the number of nomin- ations exceeds tht of the persons to be elected—not less than five nor more than fifteen, as the county council may have determined. If the papers tally with the number of councillors the candidates nominated will be declared by the chairman to be duly elected. If the nomination papers, however, are in excess of the quota of councillors, the chairman will put the names to the meeting in alphabetical order, taking a show of hands in favour of each candidate. Unless a poll is demanded the candidates who get the majerity of hands in their favour will be declared there and then as parish coun- cillors, and the election will be complete. THE POLL. If the demand for a poll is maintained it is to be held on Monday, December 17t1, "or on such day not being earlier than Saturday. the 15zh , nor later than Wednesday, the 19th December, as may for special reasons be fixed by the county council." There is neth- ing, therefore, to prevent the election being held on Saturday, provided the ceunty council agrees. The county council is also to decide upon the hours of poll- ing subject to the provision that "the poll shall always be open between the hours of six and eight in the even- ing." The clerk to the guardians will be the returning officer, and it will be left to him to determine the number and situation of the polling places, one station being provided for every 500 voters. The following is a specimen of the form of vote with which each voter will he supplied, It will be observed that the Local Govern- ment Board make a dispassinuate selection. They include the Hall, hut not the Rectory, whilst a grocer, a shoemaker, a farmer, an agricultural labourer, and a spinster stand for the general parochial interests. ELECTION OF PARISH COUNCILLORS.

BROWN (John Brown, of Water-lane, Agricultural Labourer.

GREEN (Robert Green, of Mudford, Shoemaker.)

JONES (William David Jones, of Claygate Farm, Farmer.

MERTON (Hon. Georce Travis, commonly called Viscount Merton, of Swanworth, Wilts, gentleman.)

ROBINSON (Henry Robinson, of High-street, Grocer.)

SMITH (Mary Elizabeth Smith, of Lavender Cottage, spins- ter.)

The votes are to be counted in the parish or in some place as near to it as practicable, and public notice will be given of the result. In accordance with the provi- sions of the Ballot Act,which, subject to a few modifica- tions, apply to all the polling arrangements, each candi- date may appoint an agent to attend the counting of the votes, and they will receive notice from the returning officer as to the time and place of counting. The pro- visions for maintaining the secrecy of the ballot, become the more important from the narrow compass of the electoral area, and it way he well to reproduce what the law enacts for any breach of confidence or tampering with votes : Every officer, clerk, and agent in attendance at a poll- ing station shall maintain and aid in maintaining the secrecy of the voting in such station, and shall not com- municate, except for some purpose authorised by law, before the poll is closed, to any person any information as to the nh,ma or number on the register parechial elec- tors of any elector who his or has not applied for a ballot paper or voted at that station, or as to the official mark, and no such offieer, clerk,or agent, and no person whosoever, shall interfere with or attempt to interfere with a voter when marking his vste,or otherwise attempt to obtain in the polling-station information as to the candidate for whom any voter in such statien is about to vote or has voted, or communicate at any time to any person any information obtained in a, polling-station as to the candidate for wh -in any voter in such station is about to vote or has vot-d, or as to the number on the back of the ballot paper given to any votor at such station. Every officer, clerk, and agent in attendance at the counting of the votes shall maintain and aid in maintaining the secrecy of the voting, and shall not at- tempt to ascertain at such counting the number on the back of any ballot paper, or communicate any informa- tion obtained at such counting as to the candidate for whom any vote is given in any particular ballot paper. No person shall directly or indirectly induce any voter to display his ballot paper after he shall have marked the same so as to make known to any person the name of the candidate for or against whom he has so marked his vote. Every person who acts in contravention of the provisions of this section shall be liable,on summary conviction before two justices of the peace, to imprison- ment for any term not exceeding six months, with or without hard labour. WHEN THE COUNCILLORS COME INTO OFFICE. If no poll is held for the parish or the wards of the parish the persons elected are to come into office on Thursday, December 13th. When a poll is taken the councillors will begin their duties on the last day of the year. It must be added that the rules make provision for utilising the machinery for electing parish councillors in connection with the election of district councillors, if such election should be held on the same day. In order to avoid confusion the balloting papers are to be of different colours, and if they should be placed in the wrong box the votes will not he disallowed. Should one box cnly be used there are special provisions laid down for the guidance of the returning officer in counting them. It may be feared, however, that when every precaution is taken there will be a good deal of difficulty in carrying out two elections at the same time, what- ever may be the case when the novelty has worn off.

STOCKTON HEATH-.

Tug Ctruncn INSTITUTE.—The above institute was opened for the season on Tuesday evening last in the presence of a large number of members and their friends. A social evening and smoking concert was held at which several songs, both sentimental and comic, and readings were given. During the evening the Rev. Vernon who presided,explained that the committee were obtain- ing a mission hall from the Ship Canal Company, to be used by the Institute and for general church parish pur- noses. After mentioning the offer of assistance received from the gentry in the neighbourhood, Mr. Vernon appealed to the members to do something towards the scheme themselves. This the members unanimously agreed to do by contributing a sum each week during the present session. It is perhaps as well to state that Mr. Warburton of Arley, has kindly granted a site opposite the police station at Stockton Heath, and all that now remains to be done is for the parishioners and friends to assist in raising the necessary funds to enable the committee to complete the arrangements in connec- tion with the purchase, removal and re-erection of the Hall at Stockton Heath. APPLETON PAROCHIAL COMMITTEIN.—At the fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority on Tuesday afternoon, the minutes of the last meeting of the Appleton Parochial Sanitary Committee, held on the 7th September, at the London Bridge Inn, Stcckton Heath, were read. They showed that Mr. Farrington, nuisance inspector, had produced a plan of new streets proposed to be laid out in Stockton Heath, by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co.. brewers,of Wilderspool. The plan was passed subject to Messrs. Greenall laying the main sewers in such streets, and also arranging for the said sewers to be connected with, and to discharge into, the sewer in Gaskell-st. Also that the back passages be fifteen feet wide. The committee suggested that the company should endeavour to arrange with the county authorities for the widening and straightening of the proposed street at the north-west corner of the Police Station.—The Inspector also submitted a plan of a new house and premises proposed to be erected at Stockton Heath by Mr. Ashton Hayes, of Stockton Heath, which was recommended to be passed subject to the size of the rooms being figured thereon, and a written descrip- tion furnished as required by the bye-laws.—The com- mittee decided that Mr. Percy Silcock and Mr. Farring- ton be instructed to take the necessary steps to get the sewer in Gaskell-st., Stockton Heath, extended and carried through the said street.—A letter was read from Mr. Percy Silcock containing estimate and apportion- ment of cost of the sewer in Gaskell-st.--It was resolved to defer the consideration of the subject until the next meeting of the committee.—lt was further resolved that notices be given to the owners and occupiers of houses in Gaskell-st. to make such drains as may be re- quired under the Public Health Act, 1875. In the event of non-compliance with the notices, that such work be done by the Authority,— l'he minutes were confirmed.

WARRINGTON,

A RUNCORN MAN KILLED. On Tuesday afternoon, the boiler of the steam flat "Amy," belonging to Mr. Richard Abel, of Runcorn, exploded while the vessel was en the Mersey near Monks' Ironworks,Warring- ton, causing the death of Ralph Lowe of Leicester- street, Runcorn, the engineer. The flat had just dis- charged a cargo at the chemieal works of Messrs. Pochin and Co., and was proceeding down the Mersey when the explorion occurred from some unknown cause. Lowe was badly scalded, and died shortly after admission to the Warrington Infirmary. The other occupant of the boat was uninjured.

THE INQUEST. Mr. S. Brighouse held the inquest upon the body of the deceased at the White Hart Inn, Sankey-street, yesterday morning. Mr. Fowl( r (Messrs. R. Davies, Kirkconnel and Co.) appeared on behalf of the owners of the flat" Amy." Upon the assembling of the jury, Mr. Fowler suggested that the jury visit the scene of the accident. Mr. Brighouse also was of the same opinion, and said that in his experience of those kinds of accid- ents, the jury always had a 10-tter idea m the circum- stances of the case by a visit than by any sort a de.,crip- don. After a short discussian, the jury decided to go. and one of Mr. Connor's buses was brought into requisi- tion, and the party were conveyed to Atherton's Quay. After their return, Mr. Brighouse thanked the jury for their trouble in going to inspect the flat. He thought the inspection would help them very much in the in- quiry. Peter Lowe said that deceased was his son and lived at 2, Leicester-street, Dukesfields, Runcorn. He was 23 years of ago. For five or six years, he had been a marine engineer. He was well conversant with boiler work. He had of late been engineer on board the flat" Amy belonging to Mr. Richard Abel. Witness last saw deceased alive on Sunday night, and then saw him again dead on Tuesday. Witness saw the boiler room casually on Thursday and he noticed there was very little space in the boiler room to cio any work in. Cross-examined by Mr. Fowler, witness said that he was not an engineer but was a waterman. There was not sufficient room in case of accident. • David Morgan said that he was a fitter who had been engaged five months ago onb.ard the stearr.flat " Amy." IHe then worked for Mr. Marsh, foreman on the Ship Canal at Old Quay. It was part of his duty to att to the flat. He did not examine the boiler, but five months ago he put on pressure to test the boiler. I Cross-examined by Mr. Fowler, the boiler was tested to 1001 b. pressure. He considered that that was suffici- ent for the work required. There was no leakage or the : gauge would have shown it. As a practical man, he considered that the boiler was fit for work. By Mr. Fanshaw (a juryman) : He thought the work- ing pressure was 40 or 50 lbs. In answer to further questions, he said that the boiler had been short of water from the appearance of the tubes, the upper parts of which were burnt. Thomas Gibbons, captain of the "Amy "said that the flat came from Garston Deep to Warrington on Sunday at dinner time. Ralph Lowe was on board dnring the journey. There were only two on board. Witness had nothing to do with the boiler. The flat had been work- ing continuously for ten months. Lowe had only worked on board six weeks, and a man named Darwen preceded hie as engineer. The flat lay in the river at,. Warrington from Sunday to Tuesday, and witness was on board when the accident occurred, which was about I 2.30. They were getting ready for a voyage to Runcorn ' when the accident occurred. The deceased had been on board all Monday and Tuesday. Deceased was near the engine when the explosion took place. The fire bid been alight since that morning. When the explosion occurred witness went down and found the deceased at the after side of the engine. Cross-examined by Mr. Fowler The engine was not working at the time of the accident. Witness did not look at the water glass. They put twelve or thirteen buckets full of water in the boiler on Tuesday morning. The steam gauge showed 501bs. pressure previous to the explosion. NVitness got deceased out of the engine room. By a jury : The repart was not a very loud one. The glass skylight was not blown out. Deceased's arms were scalded badly. Mr. Abel had only owned the boat since Christmas. The boat was over-hauled previous to April. Alfred Gibson, fitter, said that be knew the boiler la years ago. When first made it was put into a yacht named " Cuckoo " belonging to Mr. Chappell. The boiler was then taken to the " Beaconsfield," after lying idle for six or seven years in a yard. He had seen the boiler that morning,and was of opinion that it was short of two stays. Contraction and expansion of tubes might have caused the tube plate to crack. A boiler ought to be examined from time to tune. He could not tell whether the boiler was short of water. By a juryman: He examined the boiler previous to _ _

it being -put: into the "Beaconsfield,"and told Mr.Boscow, the owner, that he would not have it as it was so corroded. He would not have put it upon a ship to work 60 or 70Ibs. pressure. By Mr. Fowler : The plate was thick enough, if it was of good material. Re-examined by the jury: The boiler had been under

water for six or seven weeks owing to the " Cuckoo" sinking. When it was brought up again it was not repaired, but was merely cleaned. By Inspecter Spimks : The boiler was not examined by a Board of Tn./8 inspector when pit into the" Cuckow " or" Beaconsfield," hut witness did not know whether it was or not when put into the "Amy." Joseph Senior, boilermaker, said that he saw the boiler . five months ago, and retubed it, and put a stay from the back of the combustible chamber to the back end of the boiler. The boiler was then in the fiat. Witness thought that the boiler was quite sound. He had seen it since the explosion, and he believed that the plate had been overheated,whether owing to the water being below the tubes or the sediment upon the plates, he was unable to say. The iron of which it was made was superior to that made now. It was an uncommonly well made boiler. By Mr. Fowler: Be did not detect any flaw five months ago,and it was not much corroded. The working pressure was from 45 to 50 lbs., and it ought to carry double the pressure. Mr. Brighouse said that Mr. Senior's evidence quite altered the case, and he thought the inquiry might close • that day. It was his duty to see whether there was any criminal liability, but here was a man who distinctly swore that the boiler was worthy of a set of new tubes and that there was no danger whatever to work it. The accident had been brought about by someone on board being in fault. Richard Abel said that he bought the" Amy" in Janu- ary. He took the boat over from Mr. BOSCGW. The ship was then overhauled, new tubing was put in and every- thing done that was necessary. Emily Dobson, nurse at the Infirmary, said the de- ceased was admitted into the institution on Tuesday afternoon suffering from scalds. lie died three minutes afterwards. After a short deliberation, the jury decided to adjourn the inquiry until after tho Board of Trade inquiry.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES

WARRINGTON BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

THEFT FROM AN AETRENTICE. At the Borough Police Court on Wednesday, Ben Places was charged with stealing from a building in Folly-lane, on Tuesday, an oilstone value 4s. the property of Sam L. Hawthorn_ He was further charged with stealing atze shilling's worth of potatoes from a farm in Folly-lane. Prisoner pleaded guilty to the latter sharge. The chief constable in his statement to the magistrates said that the complainant was John Hawthorn, working as &joiner for Mr.CharlPs Longshaw. a builder, erecting some house in Folly-lane. On the date named at 10 o'clock in the morning, Hawthorn saw the oilstone safe in his bass, and half-an-hour later he discovered that it was gone. Information was given to the police and at half-past nine on Tuesday evening D.S. Monaghan and Sergeant Cheney (having heard tnat the prisoner was seen loitering about at the time of the theft) proceeded to his lodgings. 17, Albert-street, and Monaghan asked the prisoner if he had anything that did not belong to him. The prisoner replied that he hadn't,but Monaghan on making a search found under a heap of rags the missing oilstone in its case and the potatoes. The officers then brought the prisoner to the police station and charged him with the thefts and he replied, "I borrowed the stone this morn- ing to sharpen my razor with from Folly-lane. A lad named William Wright saw the prisoner take the pota- toes. John Hawthorn, the complainant, and D.S. Monaghan both corroborated Mr. Hunt's statement. Prisoner who pleaded hunger as the reason for the theft of the potatoes was sent; to gaol for one month with hard labour.

Asr EDUCATION CASE. —ILLEGAL ARREST. At the Warrinetou Borough Ceurt, on Tuesday,James Gorst, filecii t ter, 15,Porter-stewas charged with neglect- ing to send two children to school. The defendant did not appear, but his wife was present in his stead.—Mr. G. Riley, School Attendance Officer, prosecuted. and stated that the boys were iticorrigible, and would not attend school. He asked for the full fine to be imposed. —Mrs. Gorst stated that at three o'clock the erevious morning two police officers came to their house, and took her husband to the Police Station for the alleged non-payment of 2. 6d. due on a fine imposed some time previously. She told the offioers that the money had been paid, and showed a receipt. They took no notice of her, and brought him down to the Police Office, when the police found that no money was owing. The Chairman (Mr. Garnett) wished to know whether the woman's statement was correct.—Mr. Hunt said it seemed quite clear that a mistake had been made. The money when paid ought to have been checked in the book at the time.—lnspector James stated that the money was received at the Police Station during his absence. The officer who received it made a memoran- dum on a piece of paper, which got mislaid amongst some telegraphic forms. When the man stated that the money had been paid they found the piece of paper with the amount marked upon it.—The Chairman said he thought there should be some clear way of having the money entered aeainst persons when it was received in the absence of Inspector James.—Mr. Hunt said that in almost every case where persons were allowed time to pay the fine, the police had to go about like debt collec- torte—Mr. Riley stated that he thought his cases should , be dealt with Vize any others. Defendants were not al- lowed time to pay the lines in other cases.—The Chair- man said he was of the opinion that the fines should be paid at the time they were imposed,or not later than the following Saturday, He objected to the police going "dunning" for money.—Mr. Hunt expressed his regret to Mrs. Gorst; for the ocourrence.—One of the children was ordered to attend Holy Trinity Schools.and a fine of ss. was imposed in the case of the other boy. CHARGE Or SHOOTING A PIGEON. John Magee, Hoe ard-:t., S Inkey Bridges, was charged at the Warrineton County Petty Sessions, on Wednesday,with having on the 18th August, at Sankey, unlawfully and maliciously killed a pigeon ,value 20s.,the property of J. Earlam. Werarave,Newton-le-Willo ws. Mr. Longton, prosecuted, and Mr A. Browne, defended. Mr. Longten stated that a pigeon belonging to Mr. Joshua Earlam was taking part in the Earlestown Homing Society's race from Swindon on the date named. When the bird was nearing Sankey it was stint at by the defendant ad two others. The bird was killed and fell into the Mer,ey. Gamekeeper Irlem saw what hap- pened and picked the pigeon out of the water. Be shouted to the defendant that he knew him,a.nd that he would hear more about it. The defendant had since offered to pay the value of the bird. The Earlestown Homing Society, however, wished the case to be pro- ceeded with in order that it might be a warning to others.—John Irlam, gamekeeper for Sir Gilbert Green- all, stated that on the date named he saw the defendant and two others fire at a pigeon. There were three shots, but it was the last one that killed the bird, and that was fired by the defendant. In cross-examination witness said there were only a second between each shot. Joshua Earlam stated that the pigeon's wing produced, which was marked with his number on, was his. He valued the pigeon at £l. Robert Green. farmer, Lower Walton, gave evidence as to seeing a pigeon shot at lay three persons on the date named. He could not tell which shot killed the pigeon. Mr. Browne for the defence stated that before they could convict the defendant, it must have been shown and proved that the persons went out with the intention of killing the pigeon. He contended that such had not been proved, as a matter of fact the defendant was there for the purpose of shooting wood pigeons which be had a perfect right to do. Mr. Browne added that it was impossible for anyone to tell which shot killed the pigeon. Evidence for the defence was given by John Chadwick iron worker, 3, Bewsey-road, Joseph Smith, 6, Dale-street, Sankey Bridges and Thomas Chadwick, stating that the defendant fired the first shot. They were unable to tell which person killed the bird. The magistrates thought that there was a doubt in the case and dismissed it.

FOOTBALL
