RUGBY. WARRINGTON T. BARROW.

The team which hails from Barrow has unvariably during the last two or three seasons given Warringtcn a hard game, and there has never been much between them at the finish. On Saturday there were about 3,000 specta- tors to witness the match, and they observed a well contested struggle which kept up the traditions of the precious matches. Punctually at 330, the two teams under the charge of Mr. S. Williams of Salford entered the field, when it was discovered that they were of the

following composition. WARRINGTON: full hack, Heesom ; three quarter backs Plumpton. Evans, Potts, and Cunningham ; half backs, " Smack "Bate and Carey ; forwards. Fair Turner, Jolley, Dakiu, Taylor, Buckley, Berry, Nevins, and Thorniley. BARROW : full back, W. Sharpe ; three quarter backs. Popplewell, Kelly, T. Dickinson' and Belt; half backs Messenger, and Norttunore ; forwards, Weatherall, Hugh s, Morgan. McGerry, Woodhouse, C. Sharpe, E. Dickinson, and Satterthwaite. The game was commenced by C. Sharpe, whose kick off was well returned by Plumpton. The Barrow men at once began the rushing game and Heesom was early called on to serve. Play at length settled in the khome 25, and despite a brilliant ;passing msvement between the home threequartera, no headway could be made against the Barrovians. Carey at half back was re- peatedly collared by Northmore, and scrimmage after scrimmage took place, until Warrington were pushed back upon their lines. The Warrington forwards now recovered somewhat, and the ball was rushed to the halfway line, where Sharpe was called upon, and he re pulsed the invaders, sending them back to their quarter flag, where " Smack" Bate stopped a dangerous rush. Vp to this point, Warrington had never been over the half way line, but at length "Smack" Bate got posses- sion, and dribbled well, Thorniley also coming to his assistance. Their progress was at length brought to an end by Dickinson falling upon the ball. From the ensu- ing scrimmage, Popplewell gained possession, and by judicious kick gave his side the advantage again. A ' free kick to Barrow at this stage, made Warrington feel very uncomfortable. but McGerry made a wretched kick, which was easily repelled by Warrington who in their turn were awarded a free kick which 'Turner utili- sed to the advantage of his side. Heesom was soon again -called upon, and was not found wanting. A bout of passing between the Warrington backs, ended in Carey snaking a pass forward. From the scrimmage w's ich took place in consequence, the ball was dribbled down the field by the Warrington forwards, and upon Sharpe,the opposing fullback attempting to put a stop to it,the burly Thorniley came in contact with him with the result that he and mother earth had a very close acquain- tance. The onward rush, which aroused much enthu- siasm was not stayed until the Barrow quarter flag was reached. The attack was kept up, and Warrington ap- peared several times to be be on the point of scoring, when Plunapton received a severe injury upon the leg. The game was stopped for a short time, and Plumpton was at length obliged to leave the field, and took no further part in the match. The game was re-commenced, with Nevins drafted into the three quarters, and despite them being a man short, Warrington kept up the attack. A splendid series of passing between " Smack " Bate, Potts, Evans, and Cunningham was loudly cheered, as was also a tremendous punt by the übiquitous "Smaak" in the midst of his opponents, a minute later. The latter player was playing for all he was worth, and al- most all the passing movements originated from him. His dodgy and tricky runs frequently baffled his opponents. Warrington were at length driven back from the attack by a free kick and Heesom had great difficulty in keeping Weatherall out. At length a free kick gave Warrington relief, and by its assistance they reached half way. Numerous scrimmages took place here, .and " Smack " Bate, finding every other way blocked, astonished the Barrow men by getting between their legs, and thus by this peculiar device he gained considerable ground. It was just at this moment that Plumpton, who had been lying down in touch, left the field, and before he had reached the pavilion, Potts was hurt, and the game was delayed for a minute or two. Warrineton's fears of having to play with thirteen men were allayed however,by Potts recovering sufficiently to play. After the game again progressed,several of the for- wards,chiefly Taylor and Dakin distinguished themselves by their individual play, but bad passing spoiled their attempts. Just as the whistle sounded, " Smack" Bate got over, but from some informality, only a minor was awarded, so that half time arrived with the score, War- rineton, one minor. Barrow, nil. Turner kicked off after the interval, and it was then discovered that a fresh arrangement had been made in the Warrington team. Carey,who had not been a success as half back, was sent to his old position of three quarter and the precocious Nevins took his place as half hack. Warrington attacked immediately after the interval, and it was not long before" Smack" gave his side the lead by securing the ball which was charged down' and rushing over at the corner. The place was too difficult for Turner to further increase it to a goal. From the drop out, after a scrimmage had taken place, Taylor and Berry took the ball to the line again, but a toe strong kick sent the ball dead, and the second minor was conceded. Barrow gained ground after this, and Dickinson succeeded in eluding the whole of the War- rington players except Heesom. The latter however succeeded where the others failed, and successfully grassed his man. Heesom had been up to this time exceptionally safe, but at this point, he made an un- fortunate mistake which robbed his side of victory. The ball had been kicked by the Barrow men and Heesom with his usual sureness in his fielding secured it, In attempting to kick it, he unaccountably slipped and fell to the ground also losing possession of the ball. McGerry was up in a twinkling, and with no one to stop him, he easily scored. He was not so successful with the place kick however. The team were now on equal terms again, and each side did their best to secure the winning point. Barrow attacked strongly, and Heesom attoned for his previous blunder by some daring saves. The Barrow attack was at last repulsed by Bate making a long pass to Evans who in turn passed to Cunningham, the latter player making a long run before he was collared. Potts and Carey also in turn made beillia.nt runs without anything tangible. It was now near full time, and spectators were already commencing to leave the field, when Carey obtained the ball fr-en Potts, and with no one before him but the full back, he raced down rho field. When about to be collared by Sharpe, he kicked the ball over that player's head, and a race for it now took place between the two. Carey proved the fleeter, but Sharpe prevented him from securing the ball lay running in front of him, and the two dropped on the hall together, There were loud expressions of dissent when only a minor was awarded. The whistle blew immediately afterwards, and the game ended in a draw, with the score :—Warrington, one try, two minors Barrow, one try. WARRINGTON "A" v. WIDNES ST. MARY'S. These teams met at Widnes on Saturday when War- rington fully maintained the capital form they showed in their previous matches. O'Connor lost the toss, and kicked off up the brow. For few minutes p'ay ruled even, but in the end Warrington put in a fine passing, movement,which resulted in Edelesten scoring. Stimu- lated hv this success they continued to press the game, and in 15 minutes had scored three tries. while E Hes- ten had dropped a goal. This being but 20 minutes from the start there appeared every prospect of a tremendous score. St. Mary's now brightened up considerable, and for the remainder of the first half had certainly as much of the play as their opponents. At half time the score was—Warrington one goal, three tries, St. Mary's nil. From the restart St. Mary's showed up much better. They gradually worked their way into their opponents' territory. A line out gave them an advantage, and the Warrington back conceded a minor. From the drop out St. Marc's again got possession, and were making nicely for the line when the referee recalled them for some in- formality. Several scrimmages now ensued, and Jones receiving from one of these put in a good run, but being tackled, kicked. St. Mary's still continued to press,and when the whistle sounded were on their opponents' line. They were, however, unable to score, and the final was —Warrington one goal, three tries, St. Mary's nil. WARRINGTON Sr. MARY'S V. Sr. HELENS "A."— St. Marc's had as visitors for the second match of the season the St. Helens " A" team, and confirmed the good form displayed in their opening match by placing a win to their credit. The visitors won the toss, and elected to play with the wind. The first stage of the game saw the visitors pressing, and from a passing movement by the St. Helens backs, Warburton secured a pass intended loran opponent, outran the full back, and scored. The kick at goal failed. After this the visitors played up well, and kept St. Mary's on the de- fensive. Play settled at half-way, until half time, when the score read : St. Mary's, one try ; St. Helens, nil. On resuming, the visitors crept up the pressure, but St. Mary's defended in grand style, the three-quarters being especially careful. From the drop-out St. Helens had slightly the best of the game, and had some near shaves, when J. Simenck excepted a pass from Graham and scored, which was not improved on. At the call of time St. Mary's ran out victors of a well-contested game by two tries to one.

Pownall, Runcorn Valentine started the game, arid from the kick off sent the ball over the Runcorn goal line. After give and take play the ball was carried close to the home goal line,and Warder getting posesssion took a drop at goal, but the ball went under the crossbar and was sent dead. Then Swinton had a tun, and Valentine made several strong attempts to get over the visitors' line, hut he was too well watched to permit of scoring. Just before half- time, Johnson and Berry in turn made strong runs, but Houghton kept his lines intact, and ends were changed without any points being scored. Tne second half had not been started long before Runcorn assumed the aggressive, and for a time Swinton were hard pressed. At length Robinson got possession after a scrimmage and passed the ball wide to Richardson, who scored a try for Runcorn at the corner flag, Haughton failing at goal. After the drop-out Runcorn were penalisA for offside tactics, but from the free kick Butterworth caught the ball and dropped a goal for Runcorn from near the cen- tre (Atha field. After restarting Berry, Case and Valen- tine ran the ball te the Runcorn goal line, but the visitors, after screwing a pack, carried the ball to the other end of the field, and passing between the whole of the backs enabled Richardson to score another try for Runcorn, Houghton again failing at goal. The drop out brought no relief, and Swinton continued to be severely pressed, and but for the strong defence of Valentine, Case, Johnson and Cleminson, the visitors must have scored upon several occasions. After several tight -scrimmages had been fought on the Swinton goal line, Gaytor picked up the ball and passed to Butterworth, who upen being tackled, threw it wide to Robinson,and the latter player had no difficulty ha scoring Runcorn's third unimproved try. No side was then called,and the icose stood one goal (dropped) and three tries (13 points) to nothing in favour of Runcorn. The first portion of the game was fairly well contested, but in the second half the Swinton combination appeared to collap-re. They were outplayed at all points of the game. Tne Swinton halves were fairly smart on the ball, but their combinatian was in poor contrast to that of their rivals. At three-quarter back, Valentine tried hard to stem the strong rushes of the Runcorn forwards, while Cleminson, Case, Johnson and Berry were the best of the Swinton scrimmagers. On the other hand the Runcorn players relied upon combined play rather than individual effort, and their passing was accurate and well timed, while their forwards screwed and carried the packs in fine style. RUNCORN v. SWINTON "A " Manse—A match bet- ween these teams was played off on Saturday afternoon on the Irwell-lane enclosure, in fine weather and before a good "gate." The following were the teams :—Run- corn : Lightfoot, full back ; H. Myers, J. Nicks," T. Antrobus, and B. West, threequarter backs ; E. Wood- ward and W. Pendlebury, half-backs; H. Williams, J. Stubbs, A. Clarke, J. Moores, W.Tatlock, G.Derbyshire, S. Walker, and G. Shaw, forwards. Swinton : Berry, full back ; Tyldesley, Jackson, Greenall, and Gill, tlireequarter backs; Booth and Walsh, half-backs ; Spruce, Todd, Vicars, Kelly, Denny, Hoskey, Parry,and Jeacock, forwards. As will be seen the homesters were weak, five of them being at Swinton with the first team. In consequence of the late arrival of the visitors, a start was not made until after four o'clock, Vickars kicking off. After a little bit of aggressive play by. the Swin- tonians the Runcornians collared the ball and gave their opponents no end of trouble. A fine dribble was made by the homesters, and a struggle ensued in front of goal. Efforts to clear were of no avail, the Runeorn men tackling in fine style. Here Berry's kick out was charged down by Myers, and the ball rebounded over the line. Some good passing was afterwards seen on both sides, although the homesters proved the most effective. Half-time arrived with the score—Runcorn one try, Swinton nil. The second portion of the game was more exciting than the first. The final result was —Runcorn three tries to Swinton nothing. WIDNES v. SALFORD.-At Widnes on Saturday. before 4,000 spectators. Barber started for Widnes. Miles returned, and play settled near the centre line. Barrett made a fine run, but was pulled up near the line by Clarke. For some time play was very uninteresting, being confined to scrimmages and lines out in midfield. Then the home lines had a narrow escape through some bad play by Pooley, and Alford did a wonderful piece of play. Widnes then woke up a bit. The forwards made a combined rush, and from a scrimmage opposite the posts Barber obtained possession and dropped a fine goal. The pace became much faster after this, and each end was several times visited in quick succession, but with- out anything tangible resulting to either side. Half-time score: Widnes 1 goal (dropped), Salford nil. Play after the interval was very even, but just on time Barber again dropped a goal. Result: Widnes 2 goals (dropped), Salford nil.

SrOCKTON HEATH v. BIRKENHEAD WANDERERS "A."— Stockton Heath opened their season on Saturday, when they met the above combination on the club ground, Narrow-lane. The Wanderers having gone very strongly so far this season, a good game was anticipated, and in this respect the spectators were not disappointed. The homesters had to try three new three-quarters in Hamp- ton, Smith, and Carter. The former acquitted himself very well. and Smith did fairly well. Cal ter's capabili- ties, however, were not tested, but he seems to be in the right place. Stockton Heath losing the toss started the leather,and play soon became very fast. The homesters, were first to attack, Baldwin and Lally doing some capital work. The Wanderers were next seen working their way to the home line, and after some neat passing scored, from which a goal was placed. Barber was next applauded for several determined efforts, experiencing exceedingly hard lines. As the result of some neat pass- ing Smith was enabled to score a well judged try, the kick failing. Stockton Heath in the second half were fwaind persistently attacking, but the efforts of the visitors' full back deserved every praise. The visitors' lines had several narrow escapes, and it-was with diffi- culty that some of the combined rushes were got rid of. Higham, however, soon placed the homesters on the right side by scoring one of his noted tries, GL ver con- verting. This reverse did not seem to dishearten the Wanderers,who still played with determination. Several bouts of neat passing were next seen amongst the visitors' backs. Barber, however, watched his oppor- tunity to the second, and snatching an opponent's pass at the right time raced in amidst loud cheers, Glover again being successful at the place. One of the beat games ever witnessed on the Stockton Heath around resulted in a win for the homesters by two goals and one try to one goal.

RAILWAY SERVANTS AMBULANCE

CLASS.

PRESENTATION OF CERTIFICATES.

On Tuesday afternoon, Mr. David Meldrum, the manager of the Cheshire Lines Railway, presented the certificates and badges to the successful students of the ambulance class which was held under the tutorship of Dr. Bowden, in connection with the servants of the Cheshire Lines Committee. The ceremony took place at the goods department, and besides Mr. Meld rum, Mr. Rigby, the local manager, and Dr. Bowden were also present. Mr. MELDRIIII previous to distributing the certificates said that it gave him the greatest pleasure to come to Warrington to distribute them. It was very gratifying that the Warrington men valued a knowledge of ambulance work. Another very gratifying feature was that out of 27 vandidates who presented themselves for examination, 25 of them had been successful. That fact not, only reflected great credit upon themselves, and , showed that they had given the subject very careful attention but it also reflected very highly upon their instructor, Dr. Bowaea. It most be gratifying to that , gentleman to find that they had been so successful. It showed that he must have been very careful in his instruction. They should not be satisfied with their success however, until they had received full training in that useful work. He was sure that if the passengers who travelled on the railway knew that the railway servants bad a thorough knowledge of ambulance work, they would have a higher appreciation of them. He was quite sure that it did not need any word of his to urge upon them the importance of that good work. They would never regret it. He noticed fro-,n the list,that the men who had gained the certificates, were not of any particular grade or class, but embraced the whole of the grades. That showed that there was good feeling amongst them. lie hoped that they would not rest content with the second class certificate, but would go in for obtaining the medallion, as there were many on the other stations who had obtained them. Mr. MELnnum then proceeded t 4 hand the certificates and badges to the successful competitors, alter which in the name of the class, he presented Dr. Bowden with eight volumes of the works of Edwin Waugh, as a mark of the esteem in which he was held. Mr. Meldrum also hoped that the Warrington branch would compete for the silver cup which was given to the most success- ful class upon the C.L.C. railway. He caid that be did not see any reason why the Warrington men should not win it. Mr. RIGBY then moved and Mr. PRITCHARD seconded a vote of thanks to Mr. Meldrum for his presence that afternoen, which was accorded unanimously. Mr. MELDRIIM briefly replied and also proposed a vote of thanks to Dr. Bowden for his efficiency in getting the class to its present state of excellence.—Mr. Rican seconded the motion which was heartily accorded. Dr. I3ownEr; in reply thanked them for the vote just accorded him, and also thanked them for the present which they had given to him. The proceedings then terminated.

THE SHIP CANAL. ---- WALTON LOCK.

Walton lock is now practically finished, but it will still be a considerable time before it will be open for traffic. Ihe water from the Runcorn side of the river flows through it into the Ship Canal, but at the canal end there is a great deal of dredging and cutting to be done. At the present time there is a large dredger busily engaged deepening the channel leading from the Snip Canal to the lock. At this place where there are three outlets the water is 100 yards wide. The sluice is almost completed and in working order. It is not likely that flats, etc., can pass through the lock into the canal before the expiration ot three months owing to the large amount of clay which has to be cleared away. The work of walling in the sides of the channel is progress- ing favourably.

ASSOCIATION.

WARRINGTON V'. A-LLE-R-T-0-N—P-A-R-E. IST.—Warrington was poorly represented in this game, which was played at Allerton on Saturday. Pierpoint kicked off for Warring- ton, who began to press, and were very unlucky in not scoring, the ball hitting the cross bar. After some give- and-take play Allerton attacked, but J. Mather and Bowen relieved well. Gorton got possession of the ball, and with the assistance of Dukes took the leather up the field, where the home goalkeeper saved by putting the ball over the bar. Allerton began to look dangerous shortly afterwards, but were repulsed,but shortly before half-time they were not to be denied, and scored the only goal of the match. After half-time Warrington livened up a bit, and the game became much faster, fouls becoming very frequent. At this point Williamson showed up well, and downed his men in grand style. One of the Warrington forwards experienced very hard lines in having an apparently fair goal disallowed for off-side, the ball having been played by one of the Aller- ton backs. Each goal was visited it turn. T. Mather took the ball down the field several times, but his centres were not utilized. The game then ended, leaving Aller- ton victorious by one goal to nothing. The rick Of the Warrington team were Williamson, J. Matha, Bowen, T. Mather and Gorton. WARRINGTON ST. ELPHIN'S V. LIVERPOOL VICTORIA ATHLETIC.—This match was played on Saturday in Liverpool. A PUBLIC HOUSE ROW AT

WARRINGTON.

SINGULAR ALLEGATIONS.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Monday. Thomas Williams, engine driver, 7, Chrimes-yard, was charged with being drunk and refusing to quit the licensed premises of the" Old Crow," Fennel-st., at ten minutes to seven on Saturday night, and was further charged with assaulting the landlady, and also Patrick McLoughlin, by knocking him through a glass door.—P.C. Phillips said that at 645 on Saturday night he was at the Police Station when a message was brought that the police were wanted at the" Old Crow," Witness proceeded there in company with P.C. Millington. He saw the landlady who said she had been assaulted by the prisoner, and that when a man named McLoughlin came to her assistance, the prisoner struck him a blow,knock- ing him through the glass door of the parlour. The man's head was bleeding profusely, and there was a wound about three inches long at the back of the head. Witness sent for Dr. Peacock who put three stitches in the wound. The prisoner was drunk and he was taken into custody. He asked the landlord what the man was doing on his premises drunk, and his explanation was, that the man had been there during the afternoon and had been turned out. He had got back again, and he refused to serve him, hence the disturbance. The prisoner's sister-in-law told him that he had fallen asleep in the public-house, and was wakened by the landlady horning brown paper under his nose.—Mrs. Mather, landlady,stated that she saw the prisoner asleep on 'one of the tables in a room by himself. She asked him to go home but he used abusive language to her. Witness and prisoner's sister-in-law burned brown papet under his nose to waken him up. When he got up he struck witness and when a man came to her assistance, the prisoner knocked the man's head through a glass door.—Mary C,)nway, sister-in-law to the prisoner, gave evidence as to seeing the landlady burning paper with cayenne on it in order to rouse the prisoner. The man was so exasperated when he got up that he threatened to strike the landlady. The knocking of McLoughlin through the glass door was an accident.—Patrick McLoughlin stated that he went to the assistance of the landlady and tried to induce the prisoner to go home, when he struck witness, knocking him through the glass l door. Witness was insensible for some time and did not ! know what afterwards happened.—Mrs. Williams stated ' that she went to the "Old Crow "public-house about 1.30. He husband was there and she asked him to come home. She saw he had had enough drink and told the barmaid not to serve him. When she called again at 3.30 to bring him home she found he had been again supplied with drink. She took him home but he went back again.—Thomas Mather, landlord, said he did not see anything of the disturbance: The man was sober when he went home with his wife in the afternoon. When he returned he was not served with drink. The magistrates fined defendant ss.and costs for being drunk and 10s. and costs for the assault, and ordered him to for pay the damage to the glass door.

THE EFFECTS OF SEA AIR.

We constantly find people pondering over the value of sea air as a recuperating power. In the first place it should be pointed out that sea air is everywhere some- what moist, it is generally of relatively high density, it is always more or less in motion, and it is to some slight extent impregnated with saline particles ; it is, on the whole, much more equable in temperature than inland or mountain air. As regards its physiological action upon the healthy organism, sea air is usually tonic—i.e., it promotes the processes of nutrition t is also very generally stimulant—i.e., an excitant of the nervous system—but to this rule there are many exceptions. Warm and moist sea air, combined with conditions of local shelter, may even be decidedly sedative in character. There seem to be grounds, the " Lancet "tells us, for believing that sea air is always more or less provocative of metabolism (the performance of tte natural human function by which nourishment of the body and excre- tion of waste are affected) and tissues change in the organism. The influence of sea air upon those who ail) in ordinary health is in the great majority of cases bene- ficial. Those who are well at home experience in most cases an accession of vigour at the sea-side; those who are languid and debilitated from over-work or lone- continued strain, apart from actual illness, obtain a like benefit from sea air. The applicability of sea air to states of disease re- quires, however, rather more careful consideration. The neurotic, the hypochondriacal,the dyspeptic patient will as often get injury as benefit from such a prescription. The sufferer from cutaneous disease should, as a rule, avoid the sea. Rheumatic and gouty patients do not commonly benefit by sea air. Simple debility and anemia usually benefit by the sea air, The freshness and purity of the air, the breeziness, and the large aver- age of sunlight are a sufficient explanation for this. It is a matter of common observation that the nervous system, in some cases, is rendered more irritable by sea air, with resulting insomnia, nervous debility, and the like; while in other cases sea air produces unusually deep and refreshing sleep, and a general sense of" bien etre." In some cases, again, the effect of sea air is at first excitant and subsequently sedative. Much depends upon the special characteristics of any particular resort, prevailing winds, shelter, /cc. The safe general rule seems to be that where there is much irritability, particularly of the sort that may be termed " irritable weakness," sea air disagrees. Where the nervous system is simply torpid or debilitated, with- out decided irritability, sea air will often be found beneficial. Another broad rule that may help in decid- ing for or against the seaside is the well-known fact that all inflammatory conditions tend to be aggravated by sea air.

BRITISH HEROES.

" The Downfall of Lobengula," by W. A. Wills and L. T. Collingridge, contains a description of the late of Major Wilson and his party,one of the saddest and most heroic incidents in British history. To the end there was no thought of surrender, no re- quest for quarter. Taking cover behind the dead bodies of their horses, the little party fought for two long hours. Again and again the Matabeli would issue from their cover, and again and again were repulsed with a well-directed fire. But at last the end came "Of the thirty-four valiant men whose hearts beat high with hope and courage as they rode behind their leader in the early dawn that morning, only one re- mained erect ; the rest lay prone, dead or dying, upon that field of honour. " The name of the one man who stood at bay against art army of Matabeli will never be known; his remains could not be identified. But the natives tell that, picking up several rifles and bandoliers, this hero amongst heroes made his way to an ant heap some twenty yards from where the rest lay stretched upon the earth. "From that point of vantage he checked, single- handed, several rushes of the Matabeli with a cool and deadly fire. At length, shot through the hips, he sank on his knees, but continued to load and fire until he succumbed to his wounds. "Then, and not till then, the Matabeli came out from the bush, but on reaching the hallowed circle, where the patrol lay side by side, were fired upon by several of the unconquerable wounded who were still alive. 'So great had been the terror and demoralisation inspired by the desperate bravery of the patrol, that when the revolvers rang- out the natives turned and fled precipitately into the bush ; and it wa.s not till several hours later—when the sun was right overhead, as the MaZabeli tell the tale—that they again ventured to leave their cover. But by this time death had mercifully comet' the wounded, and as the native warriors gazed upon the forms of their fallen foes there was silence. " For some weeks afterwards the comrades of these brave men had hoped against hope that even one of the patrol might have escaped down the Shangani River, al- though, as Ingram, the American scout, had explained, Two of the men were dismountrA, and many of the horses were completely done up. Some of those with the best mounts might have got away, but—well, they were not the sort of men to leave their chums.' He added, ; I guvss they fought it out right there— where they stood.' rhus it was ; for when Dawson crossed the river some weeks afterwards, the bones of thirty-three of Wilson's heroic band were found laying close together, even as they had stood at the last supreme moment. Those of the dauntless man who had faced the Matabeli alone lay apart."

Owing to the refusal of the manufacturers to grant the Blackburn list to the lootners and drawers, it is feared that the whole of the cotton mills in Preston will have to step. Lord Rosebery, asked by a correspon lent if he thinks the parish council elections should be conducted apart from political considerations, says that would be the most desit able course in the abstract, but he is of opin- fon that the matter will be decided by the localities themselves. He points out that in some districts politics find a place in municipal elections, and asserts that it is impossible to lay down a gelteral rule.

tetteto to Mr Otittor.

• THE FOOD OF THE PEOPLE. Sir,—lt has again and again been pointed out that a substantial meat can be prepared tor which a simple halfpenny shall cover the cost ot the ingredients. Now, although I wholly depre.cate any attempt to lower the subsistence minimum, and thereby tend to reduce the minimum on which some wages are calculated, I feel that, none the less, a fact is always an important item of knowledge to obtain, and that it the fact of the proper mode of economical nutrition were more widely known, we should not only have less distress in the homes of the poor, but less disease also. If the scanty earnings are spent on cheap flesh meat or fish, the parts purchased will be either the offal or the best parts in a stale state. Either of these will be injuri- ous to the habitual consumption of cheap and improper food by the poor, is manifest in the various forms of disease which affect them, and which appear in our con- tinuous round in the out-patient department of every Large hosp:tal. Mal-nutrition lies at the root of an enor- mous number of diseases which decimate the poor— especially the women and their weakly off-spring. I venture by your courtesy to point out that (1) every- one should eat of the best goods ; that (2) grains (e.g. wheat, oats, maize) used in puddings and porridges : pulses (e.g. lentils, beans, peas) used in soups, savouries and puddings; fruit (e.g. dates, raisins, apples) eaten with bread, at prices ranging from id. to 4d. per lb.; ripe, mature and sound, er_ wn under the full sunlight and in the pure air of the Southern lands, are far more healthy, more nutritious and more beneficial to the con- sumers, than the fl-sh of animals, fed in semi-darkness, fattened on a surfeit of oily goods, deprived of exercise and then killed to prevent them dying at the moment when their own fat was on the point of suffocating them. In the interests of the health and happiness of the community I venture to draw-attention tothe important social fact that everyone should eat of the best ot every- ' thing, and that this can be done it food can be derivea from the vegetable kingdom. I do not give advice which I am afraid to adopt mrself,for I have been tor ten years a strict vegetarian, with marked benefit. Your readers should send P. ddreFsf-d wrapper for some free literature to the office of "The liegJarian " Memor- ial Hall, Farringdon-street, E.C., or they might do well to enclose a small postal order for cookery books and for literature to enlighten beginners in the better pathway of diet.—Yours truly, . JOSIAII OLDFIELD, 11.1.8.C.L.

EARLBSTOWN V. LElGH.—Earlestown finished a very successful season at Leigh on Saturday by a creditable vistory over their old opp, rents Leigh. Winning the toss the home players commenced batting on a wicket that was still wet with the previous night's heavy dew, necessitating, the frequent use of sawdust. Bouth and Bunn made a capital star, the partnership yielding 44 runs, but with four addod two other wickets fell. Stewart then made a useful stand, which yielded 24. Of the rest Hindley alone succeeded in reaching double figures, the sum total of the innings being 110. The usual pair starttd batting f.r the visitors, but with only nine on the hoard, Rushand unfortunately played a ball into his wieke, s. Clare being dismisse,l in rapid succession. ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. The match between Warrington and Barrow on Satur- day was a very unsatisfactory one, for Warrington had to play the greater part of the first hall, and the whole ' of the last half with only fourteen men. Taking this into consideration a draw is not very bad alter all. A great many people considered also that Carey ought to I have been awarded a try at the close of the match, in- I stead (f)f only a minor. It was a very near thing, and Carey had distinctly hard lines in not being able to score owing to the Barrow full back practicaily obstructing him. The Warringtonians have not yet won a victory in the Wilderspool enclosure this seas,m, hut as they have for several seasons been rather indifferent starters, there is nothing to be alarmed at.

Since the season when Warrington gave the Barrow men such a tremendous beating, the two teams have not , been able to do very much with each other. Warrington have had the majority of wins. for Barrow have only a solitary one to boast of, but the wins have been by nar- row majorities. Last year the first match which was played at home, and which wa a league contest, War- rington were victorious by two trios to one. At the re- turn match,the rnsult was a try to nil In favour of War- rington. It is therefore fully evident that Barrow play their best against Warrington. Warrington were dis- tinctly unfortunate in the match OM Saturday, for early in the game Plumpton, who made his debut to the War- rington public in the present season, was compelled to leave the field owing to a severe injury upon his leg. Notwithstanding this fact, they had quite as much of the game as the Barrovians, and the try the latter ob- tained was a most lucky one. It is very rarely that a team finds Heesom napping.

Coming to the players,notwithstanding the blunder he made, Fleesom played extremely well. It is quite un- accountable however he lost his footing in tlt. manner he did, for he is usually so surefooted. The best of men make mistakes sometimes, and it was Heesom's turn on Saturday. However it was his only tniqake, and the rest of his play was almost faultless. His vis-i-vis Sharpe also did justice to his name, for he was veaj, sure in his tackling and fielding. Among the three- quarters, Plumpton showed great promise until his acci- dent, while Evans was also as fearless as ever. Potts did not do anything out of the common in the match. On the other side Popplewell and D:ekinson were es- pecially prominent. "Smack " Bate was without doubt the best half back if not the best player on the field. He played up to his very best form, and fr -quenters 9f the football enclosure at Wilderspool know what that means. Carey was given a trial at half ba,:k, but he was not a success, and when put to his own post at three-quarters again he was far more useful. The Barrow team had also a smart pair of halves in Northtnore and Messenger. Among the home forwards, Turner, Jolley, and Berry were the leading lights.

Nevins deserves a paragraph all to himself. This player entered the football field for the first time this season, and his first position was that of a forward. When Plimpton was injured, Nevins was sent to fill his place at three-quarter bpck, and after half time he was again removed to the half hack divisicn in Carcy's place. Had neesom kindly obliged he would probably have gone full back, and thus filled every position in the team. It is not everyone who can do justice to so many positions as Nevins can, and the team is fortunate in possessing such a many sided player.

--- To-day the Warrington team ent2r upon the league contests, when they will lae favoured with a visit from the Rochdale Hornets. The following is the tam selected to represent Warrington in this match :—Full back. Heesom ; three-quarter backs, Carey, Potts,Evans, and Cunningham ; half tacks. " Smack " Bate, and Clarke ; forwards, Fair Turner, Jolley, Buckley, Taylor, Berry, Dakin, Nevins, and Thorniley.

---- - Warrington should account for the Rochdale team pretty easily.for this team have not been doing anything extraordinary so farvand last SAturday they had to sub- mit to a crushing defeat on their own ground, at the hands of Leeds. Last year the match at Warrington with the Hornets resulted in a somewhat lucky victory for the " wirepullers" by a try to nil, but in the return , at Rochdale the home team were defeated by a goal and a try to a try. The Rochda2e Hornets' stiength has generally been supposed to lie in their forwards, 1 but I hear that this year they have not so good a lot as last year.

-- The " A " team as usual came out on the riga side in their match with Widnes St. Mary's, whom they beat by one goal three tries to nil. The team has yet to ac- knowledge defeat, and they have not even a draw to mar their record. To-day they j-)urney to the heiOits of Mosley to eng,age the Mossley Brow team. They have a good team to uphold thAr honour, as it is of the fol- lowing composition :—Full hack, Busc.ow ; threrquarter backs, Edelsten, Lawless, Mannion, and F. Harnpson ; half backs, John T. Bate and Foden ; forwards, Hough- ton, Bailey, Burrows, Savage, Randalls, Andrews, Fair- hurst, and Wilcocks.

The junior teams of the district were flying at high game on Saturday. For instance, Stcckton Heath took on the Birkenhead Wanderers "A" and defeated them to the tune of two goals and a try to a goal. It is pretty evident that Stockton Heath are very strong this season. They have a very good lot of players, some of whom have played with the Warringt in first team. St Mary's also defeated St. Helens 'A' on Siturday by two tries to a try at Mill-lane. The Warringt,n cup-holders will, without doubt, give as good an accouat of themselves as they did labt year.

On Wednesday night, a meeting of the Lancashire County Football Club was held, to elect the committee for the present season. The following were the persons elected together with the number of votes they received : Northern division (one member) J. M. Mawson, Barrow, ; Southern division (four members), J H. Smith, Widnes, 67 ; J. Higson, Salford, 54 ; J. E. Warren, War , rington, 51; S. E. Wilson, Liverpool, 38 ; N.qth-eastern division (three members). E. Healey, Rochdale Hornets, 55; J. Saville, Oldham, 51; W. Lees, Walk:len and S% Helens, 33; North-wesrern division (four inembersl, J. W. Clegg, Wigan, 78; J. Mills, Swinton, 75; J. Rob- ertson, Broughton, 59; J. C. Poyser, Broughton Rangers, 58.

It was finally decided tl commence a championship competition for a cop, for the " A !' teams of the mem- bers of the first league in the Lancahire Rugby Com- petition. IVarrington "A" will thr•r•foro play their first league match on Saturday next wizh Oldham "A at Warrington. •

The Committee also gave their decision with reference to the charge of professionalism aninst the Leigh club. The proceedings were private, but- the Chairman (Mr. F. A. Grover) eventually announced that the club would be suspended until the end of Novernb,r. This covers all last year's players. Mr. Wright, on behalf of the Leigh Club, seated that an appeal would be lodged with the Rugby Union against the Committee's decision. He allo asked whether the club would be allowed to play pend- ing the decision of the Rugby Uirion. The Chairman replied that the question was one with Which that Com- mittee had nothing to do. In reply to a further ques- tion, the Chairman said the Committee would hold a special meeting within the next week to consider the question whether, the Leigh Club should he allowed to play off the League fistures they had arranged between now and the end of November.after the term of suspen- sion had expired.

I am glad to be able to state that the suspension of the I Leigh club will not interfere at all with the Warrington fixtures. The first meeting of the two teams is fixed for December Ist. at Leigh, and will therefore he the first match which the Leigh club will play after the period of their suspension expires. The following is the team which will represent Roch- dale Hornets ;--full back, Tweedale ; three quarter backs,WightmAn, llatljwell, Bowker, and liherwood ; half backs ; Marshall, and Channyin ; forwards, Ash worth, Butterworth, Dee, Horsfield, Simpson, Melledew- Trevor, and Piggot.

CRICKET.

Warrington 2nd team concluded their last match on Saturday by making a draw with Winnington Park at Winnington. There was some od play on both si,les, Clarke with 43 not out being the highest scorer. After engaging in the field, the teams met together at the Crown and Anchor, Northwich, where, after tea was , partaken of, a jolly evening was spent ,and the members broke up late in the evening, and Warrington wa4 reached about 10.10 p.m. After this note I don't expect any more cricket notes tor a few months to come.

CYCLING. Those very good friends, the Warrington and Widnes Cycling Clubs, to the number of 37, carried out a most successful inter fixture on Saturday last, after witness- ing and assisting their friends of the Hough Green Cycl- ing Club in their 25 miles handicap they rode on to Crouton where football was indulged in, after which a tnost excellent tea was provided at the " Unicorn " to which the cyclists signified their approval in the usual way. A smoking concert at which such artists as Messrs Mounfield, Whittle, Whitham, Beams, Buxton, Taylor and others of greater rminence ably assisted, bringing to a close a very enjoyable outing at about 9.20.

ATHLETICS. I notice that a Warringtouian has been achieving \ honours in pedestrianism at the Lee Brook Grounds, Wednesburv. I cull the following from a contem- porary :—‘l Probably one of the best framed pedestrian handicaps launched in the Midlands for many years was that brought to a conclusion at-the Lee Brook Grounds, Wednesbury on Tuesday. The promoters were the Midland Pedestrian Company, who gave the sum of £5O to be competed for in an All-England 135 Yards Foot Handicap. The final, heat, it will be remembered, was run late on Monday night last, when the excited crowd broke into the enclosure and swarnied round the referee, the followers of each man claiming the race. The referee, seeing how matters stood, wisely declared it no race,and ordered the final to be run again on Tuesday, when another large and enthusiastic company assembled Betting ruled at evens Green (Westhoughton), 5 to 4 against Green (Warringtr)n), 6 t 3 1 Crovden, and 8 to 1 Bruton. According to Monday night's final another close and exciting struggle was anticipated. and so it proved. Result :—Green, Warrington, 17-. (£4O), 1; T. Green, Westhoughton, 131- (£6), 2; F. Bruton, Dudley, 15 (£2 103.), 3 • S. J. Croydon, Sed.geley, 20 (103.), 4. A grand struggle took place, the Warrington ped. just holding his own, and winning on the post ; nine inches divided second and third; and half a yard separated third and fourth. W. Lewis, of Birmingham, was starter, and E. Firm, of Darlaston, referee."

Thomas Green,who tiPs been so succes4ul,commenced his "running " career 15 years ago, when he came in third, in a 220 yards handicap, in the Warrington Work- ingmen's club sports. Since then he has won numerous prizes. He woa so many events, that at length he determined to become a professional. After severd failures, he was at length matched with Walsh for £25 a side for the championship of Warrington. Green won the match very easily, and judging from his feat on Saturday, he is still keeping up his good form.

RUNCORN FREE LIBRARY COMMITTEE.

THE LIBRARIAN'S REPORT OF THE BELFAST

CON FERENCE.

On Tuesday evening the usual monthly meeting of the Runcorn Free Library Committee was held in the Board room of the Town Hall, Mr. IN . -Handley in the chair. There were also present the Rev. John Simpson, Messrs. H. J. Grice, S. Pope, T. Riley, W. llankinson, and J. D. Jones, librarian and secretary. Mr. Jones submitted his cash statement for the past month showing that the receipts had been £sl3s. 91, of which £5O was from the id. rate. The expenditure during the same period amounted to £l9 18. 3d. The report stated that the library had been open 21. days, in the course of which period a total of 1,734 volumes bad been issued, classified as follows :—Lending department: Theology, 29; philosophy, 2; science, 61; history, Sze;., 122; poetry, 17; ficton, 1,31.4; miscellaneous, 138. Reterence department: Miscellaneous, Cl. These figures,' show an increase of 187 in the lending department and 6 in the reference department.—The cash statement and 4 report were adopted.—The Librarian further stated that during the month there had been presented to the + library :—By the Rev. John Simpson, "Minutes of the Wesleyan Conference, 1894 " ; anonymously, a volume of Tacitus," printed at the Elzevir Press, 1672; Mr. James Wildidg (the author), "Building stones used in Liver- pool," being a paper read before the Liverpool Geological S ,ciety ; Mr. S. M. Temperly, a copy of the "Runcorn Free Press? November 9th, 1867.—The Chairman said be presumed that letters of thanks had been forwarded to these donors to which Mr. Jones answered in the affirmative.

TIM BELFAST CONFERENCE. Mr. Jones then presented his report of the " Armin, meeting of the Library Association of the United King- dom held at Belfast, September 4th, sth, 6th, and 7th, 1894." Beneath are a few extracts from the report. “The programme contained a greater number of papers than that of any other meetings since the formation of the Associlation, and dealt with library history, biblio- graphy, library management, and library legislation, besides several papers of a miscellaneous character.' "Mr. Campbell, of the British Museum, contributed an extremely technical paper on The Bibliography of the Future.' He advocated the establishment of a National Bibliographical Bureau, in connection with which there should be numerous societies for the preparation of spe- cial bibliographies, so that every book in Great Britain and Ireland should be properly catalogued, and every article in periodical literature duly indexed. Experts found food for thought in this paper, but your librarian confesses that he was very soon hopelessly lost in the labyrinthine maze of Mr. Campbell's multifarious propo- sals." kenwell open library provoked a most animated discus- sion. It appears that after mature consideration the , Library Commissioners of Clerkenwell decided to admit the public to the shelves to select their own basks. The public are satisfied with the change, and the Commissioners say they would on no account go back to the old system. Several oppon- ents of the plan were inclined to view it with favour when fully laid before them, and the safeguards indi- cated. Is only remains for a new library to begin on the open access principle for it to become pretty general." " Mr. Quinn's paper on the classification of books for libraries in which readers are allowed access to the shelves, was meant to meet one ot the difficulties of the open library system. And a very good system of classifying it seemed to be; the object being to enable the borrower to find what he wants with as little trouble as possible—by baying the works grouped accoraing to their subjects,and not according to their accession number as is usually the case." "Dr Garnett and Mr. Mayhew,of the British Museum, contri- buted papers on Pivot presses at the British Museum,' and On the extension of space in libraries.' The pivot presses are a new ingenious improvement on the well- known sliding presses of the British Museum, and there is little doubt that they provide a means of extendine almost indefinitely the beok accommodation of any library." " Miss James, late librarian of the People's Palace. London, rdad a short but suggestive paper on Boston Home Libraries.' This is a system by which small libraries of from 20 to 30 volumes are lent out for longer or shorter periods so that families can have the advantage of complete courses of recreative and studi- ous reading. The report then went on to describe a paper read by Mr. Briscoe' of the Nottingham Libraries, explanatory of a system formulated to cultivate the tastes of readers who required guidance in the selection of books. The scheme which had " werked very advan- tageously and was highly appreciated throughout Not- tingham," was that of delivering half-hour lectures, the subjects dealt with being individual writers in various departments of literature, single books, and groups of authors. " The lectures were reported briefly-in the local press which added to the popularity of the scheme. Many workinemen had personally expressed their grati- tude for the assistance afforded them in their reading." An exhibition of library appliances, in which every novelty of library mechanism was displayed, proved a very instructive part of the proceedings." 'the Chairman remarked that after listening to the report read by their Librarian everyone would be satis- fied that the Committee had acted wisely in sending him to the Conference to represent them. He thought there had been a misconception in the minds of some of the Commissioners at the last meeting when their action in sending Mr. Jones was criticized. They had sent him to Belfast in the belief that they were acting in the public interest. One thing he might mention was that they had not trespassed to the extent of a single penny on the Library rate. They had received a special annual subscription which be (the Chairman)thou,ght was under the control of the Committee. He hoped that the public would share with the members of the Committee in their opinion that Mr. Jones was sent for the purpose of gaining knowledge for the benefit of the Library frequenters. Mr. Once observed that as a member of the Commit- tee he must express himself as very much pleased and highly satisfied with the result of Mr. Jones' visit to Bel- fast. He (the speaker) was very sorry he was not present at the last meeting of the Commissioners, and also when the Committee decided to send Mr. Jones. If he had been present at the Commissioner's meeting he should have strongly supported Mr. Hankinsen who was, on that occasion almost alone. He was fully satisfied. that it was in the interests of the reading public of Run- corn that Mr. Jones was commissioned by them to represent the Library at the Conference and whatever information Mr. Jones had gained he would surely use it to the advantage of the readers. Mr. Pope thought that the visit of Mr. Jones would bear fruit some day and benefit the patrons of the Library for many years. If the committee had done anything contrary to the rights as a Library Committee, it had been done inadvertently. He did not think that the Commissioners, according to the newspaper reports, had objected to sending Mr. Jones; but because they were not consulted. That was a small matter, and, in his opinion they were justified in protesting ; but he thought that in the future the committee would take care' not to do anything beyond their power. Mr. Hankinson also expressed the opinion that the Commissioners were not opposed to Mr. Jones represent- ing the committee at Belfast, but to the committee hav- ing decided to send him before that resolution had been approved by them. They had termed it an irregularity, but had the committee waited until the Commissioners met again it would have been too late for Mr. Jones to

go. The Rev. J. Simpson said he was sorry he had not been present at the previous meeting of the committee for he should certainly have voted in favour of sending Mr. Jones. Probably at the next Commissioners' meeting the Chairman (Mr. Handley) would set the matter right. The rev. gentleman personally thanked Mr. Jones for the interesting report he had read over. The Chairman intimated that he would do his best to satisfactorily explain the matter if it was brought up at the next meeting of the Commissioners.

RUNCORN AND DISTRICT STARR- BOWKETT BUILDING SOCIETY.

'The annual meeting of the members of the Run- corn and District 406th Starr-Bowkett Building Society was held on Monday evening at the offices, 34, Bridge-street. Mr. James Povah, president, occupied the chair, and amongst those present were Messrs. A. Riley, J. Poole, T. Riley, J. M. Frith, T. Booth, James Lea, Joseph Stubbs, F. L. Scott,Joseph Marsh and William Handley, secretary. THIRTEENTH ANNUAL REPORT. 'Me Chairman submitted the thirteenth annual report of the directors for the consideration of the members. It ran as follows :—During the year, the sum of £432 5-2. has been received as members' subscriptions, and the repayments on mortgages have yielded £779 18s. 2d. There has also been received £l4 as commission on trans- fer of appropriations. Fines, fees, interest on advances to members, and bank interest have yielded £23 us. 2d., which ,with the balance of £337 24. 81, brow:tit forward from last year, makes an income of £1,572 17s. 'The ex- penditure on account of working e-xpenses for the year amounted to £47 Bs. 10d., a reduction upon last year of £3 ss. id. A nett surr. of £570 10s. Bd. has been paid during the year to withdrawing members and death claims, and £650 has been advanced this year to mem- bers on mortgage of property. The balance in favour of the society alter writing off the usual amount for depre- ciation is £lO2 12s. 11d. The balance at the bank at the making up of the accounts being £304 17s. 61. Owing to a tarp proportion of the income being required to try-et appropriations and withdrawals, only one appro- priation has been made during the year, as follows : May 21st, 1894, Reg. No. 197, £2OO. Making a total number of 70 appropriations from the commencement of the society. Several deferred appropriations have been taken up during the year, secured on properties of ex- ceptional value. The mortgage account shows that many of the advances have been steadily reduced,several being nearly raid off. The whole of the properties are in good state of repair, much above the value secured by mort- gage to the society, and where not occupied by a mem- ber, well tenanted. It is satisfactory to state that no property has ever been thrown on the hands of the society,a strong testimony to the value of Starr-Bowkett Societies in promoting habits of thrift and providence. Twenty-three members (including one death claim) have during the year under notice of withdrawal received back their subscriptions, less fines and working ex- penses; the membership at the close of the year being ' 230. Messrs. Beckett and Unsworth having retired from their offices as trustees, the directors recommend the ap- pointment of Mcssrs. Povah and Poole to fill the vacan- cies. The auditors have carefully examined the deeds and securities of this society, and have certified to the correctness of the accounts. On the proposition of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. T. Riley, the report and balance sheet were adopted, Messrs. royal) and Poole being elected trustees in the places of Messrs. Beckett and Unsworth. Mr. J. R. Frith was re-elected the third trustee. The retiring directors were Messrs. J. M. Frith, J. Povah, A. Riley, and F. L. Scott, who being eligible for Ire-election were again chosen directors. Messrs. W. Riley and T. Dean, auditors, were re- appointed. After the Secretary's salary had been re-adjusted in the usual way, a vote of thanks was accorded the Chairman and Directors. and the proceedings closed.

FRODgHAM.

WOC.DHOUSES.—Very successful harvest thanksgiving services were held in connection with the Primitive Methodist Society at this place on Sunday. Mr. R. Pea- cock and Misses Wright and Vernon, of Runcorn, con- ducted the services on the Sunday. Toe Rev. H. R. Dideock also preached on the Monday evening. The total proceeds were £2 03. 4d. HARVEST FESTIVAL.—HarVeSt thanksgiving services were conducted on Sunday in the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Frodsham, sermons being preached by the Rev. J. Didcock. A large quantity of flowers, fruit and vege- tables was tastefully arranged in different parts of the sacred edifice. The congregations were large, and the special hymns and anthems rendered by the choristers was much enjoyed. PROVIDENTIAL ESCAPE or A STEEPLE-JACK. —Whilst working at the steeple of the Trinity Wesleyan Chapel, Frodsham, when fully fifty feet from the ground, on Saturday morning, a workman had a narrow escape of death. One of the cords of the swing seat became un- fastened and the man fell a distance of fourteen feet,but fortunately he succeeded in catching hold of a rope and hung on to it until rescued from his perilous position. LECTURE.—Mr. J. S. Gordon, B.Sc., one of the County council lecturers,delivered the first of a series of lectures on agricultural subjects in the Girls' School, Frodsham, on Friday evening. There was a good attendance, and the Rev. H. B. Bloge,-, vicar, presided. The Lecturer dealt with " Breeding and management of dairy stock," in a very instructive manner. He was asked several questions at the finish and 'celve satisfactory answers. THEFT OF A WATC/I.—AtFrodsham Police Court, on Monday, before Messrs. C. Reynolds and A. Thomas, James Phillips, of Wigan, was charged with stealing a watch and chain valued 305., from the person of John Durkin, at Dunham-o'-th-Bill, on the 15th inst. From the evidence it appeared that the parties were drinking together on the Saturday, ani prosecutor,after awaking from a sleep missed his watch and chain. The police were inforre ed of the affair, and when Consrable Manley spoke to Phillips of the matter he said he knew nothing ,but it. Being told, however, by the officer that he would have to go to the Police Station, he confessed to taking the articles from Durkin for safety and to placing them under a bed at Mr. Booth's. Whilst going to this house prisoner tried to run away, but the constable kept him in view and saw him take something from his pocket and place it between a hamper and some boxes. On looking there the watch and chain were found. Prisoner was fined 103. and costs, or an alternative of 21 days in gaol.

HALTON.

- - - HARVEST THANKSGIVING.—OR Sunday morning and evening and Monday evening harvest thanksgiving ser- vices were conducted at the Halton Wesleyan Church. The interior was prettily decorated with the, usual offer- ings, some exceptionally fine samples of produce being visible. The Rev.W. George preached on Sunday morn- ing and evening, whilst on Monday evening the resident minister, the Rev. W. Lile Stone, occupied the pulpit. Special anthems were rendered by the choir and highly appreciated by the large congregations which assembled. The collections were in aid of the trust. fund. MARRIAGE OF THE Ray. J. LOCKWOOD, LATE OF HALTON .—At the Kilhampton Parish Churcn, Cornwall, on Thursday, was celebrated the marriage of the Rev. J. Lockwood, late Vicar of Halton, to Miss Theodore Cole, eldest daughter of Mr. E. Cole, of Cricket St. Thomas, and formerly of Brendon, Thornbury, Devon. The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a travelling dress of grey and blue cloth, with silk and silver trimmings, blue silk bonnet to match,and a spray of orange blossoms. Miss May Cole acted as bridesmaid, and Mr. Thoma 3 Bartlett as best man. The Rev. Lyde Hunt,vicar of Poughill,officiated in the absence of Canon Thynne, who is in Scotland. The church bells rang a merry peal in honour of the event, Mr. Cole being well- known and highly respected in the neighbourhood. The happy pair proceeded to Southampton and sailed for .1 America in the steamship City of New York on the Bth I inst. The presents were numerous and valuable.

GOOD TEMPLARY AT LATCITFORD. The usual session of the Bonehill Branch Lodge of the Independent Order of Good Templars was held in the Temperance Hall. on Monday evening. under the presidency of Bro. Boscoe and A. Ford supported the proposition after which Bro. J. Ditchfield suitably responded. On Tuesday evening a further paper was read by the same Brother to the Young Men's Bible Class. .

A BATCH OF CASES. At the Warriagton County Petty Sessions, on Wednesday,a nutkber of persons were summoned at the instance of nspector Spencer, inspector of weights and meastres,for having in use unjust and

unstamped weights and scales. Mr. J. M. Porter, fisi) dealer, Warrington, was sum- moned for having on the 7th September, at Sankey, had in his possession for usF, in trad, a number of weights ranging from 71bs. to 2.)2.1., which were unjust,and also with having at the same time and place several weights which wete unstamped. Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, re- presented the defendant. Inspector Spencer stated that he saw a youth in the employ of Mr. Porter selling fish at Sankey Bridges. He examined the weights and found 71bs. weight 3 drachms Unlit, 21bs. 2 drachms; 11b., IA drachms; lb., 1 drachm lb., 1 drachm; 2 z.. 1 drachm. The whole ot the weights with the exception of the Ms. weight were also unstauiped. For the de- fence, Mr. Browne said Mr. Porter was under the im- pression that every weight in his place had been stamped by Mr. Place, market inspector, whom he had subpcened as a witness. If there were any unstamped they did not know where they had come from—Mr. Place stated that Mr. Porter sent his weights every three months to be stamped. Only three of the weights produced were unstamped. Fish dealers' weights be-Ame light very quickly through corrosion.—A fine and 2s. 6d. and costs was imposed. John Caldwell, farmer, Orford, was charged with having two 561bs. one 71bs. weight, and two 41bs. weights, which were unjust, and was further charged with having them in his possession unstamped. Mr. Unsworth, solicitor, represented the defendant. Inspec- tor Spencer proved the case, and stated that the weights were from a *lb. to 3oz. light. They had not been examined for years. For the detence Mr. Unsworth stated the weights were used in the weighing of potatoes, and farmers were not particular to a pound weight in potatoes. Mr. Caldwell was perfectly ignorant that the weights vverelight.—He was fined 53 and costs. Emma Parsonage. Rixton, pleaded not guilty to hav- ing in her possessi-n for use in trade one 561bs. and one 281bs. weight, which were unjust. Inspector Spencer stated that the 561bs. weight was /11).light,and the 231bs. weight 6oz. light. The weights were on the scales at the time of his visit.—The defendant denied that tee weights were ever brought into use.—She was fined 53. and costs, and the weights were forfeited. Messrs. Carter and Sons, bakers and flour dealers. Bridge-st., Warrington. were summoned for having on the 7th September, at Penketh, in their possession cer- tain unjust weights, and also an unstamped pair of scales. Inspector Spencer stated that on the date men- tioned he met one of Messrs. Carter's bread vans at Pen- keth. He raw a man delivering bread. Witness asked him if he had scales and weights, to which the man re- plied in the affirmative. Be asked him to produce them, and witness found a 41bs.weight three drachms light and a 21bs. weight two drachms light. The scales were per- fectly correct but were unstamped. Mr. Carter stated that for same time he had had an arrangement with a first-class firm to keep his scales in order. He would hand to the magistrates the contract. When the firm examined the scales they must not have noticed that the one in question was unstamped. Be had written to them on the matter and they had apologised to him for the mistake. The weights became light owing to fric- tion caused by being in a box in the bread van.—The magistrates fined the defendant 2s. 61. and costs. William Pickston, coal dealer, Smiley Bridges, was charged with having in his possession for use in trade, a scale which was unstansped. Inspector Spencer said he found the defendant's coal scale unstamped. lie had evidently been using it for four years without having it stamped. The defendant stated that he took the scales to Warrington and Superintendent Weir told him to repair it and he would then come and stamp it. No one however, came.—He was fiiy. t 2s. 61. including costs.

WARRINGTON.

WORKINGMEN'S GOSPEL TEMPERANCE MISSION

_ HALL. There was a gospel t7tmperance meeting in the above-named hall on Sunday, presided over by Mc. J. Drury. Interesting addresses were given by the Chairman, Mr. A. Horrocks, Mr. Thomas Woolley, Mr. 3. V. Tanner,and Mr. Green. The meeting was well attended. FIRE IN A DWELLING-HOUSE. A small outbreak of tire occurred at 45, Cart- wright-street at 1-45 p.m. on Monday. Some rags in a cupboard close to the fireplace had become ignited, there being no one in the house at the time but a baby. A caller, on opening the dot-r was surprised to see .dense volumes of smoke issuing from the cupboard. He at once raised an alarm. The baby was rescued, and the rags were thrown out of the house. Very little damage was done. P.S.A. AT THE Y,M.O.A. Mr. 11. M. Ashton took the chair at the above meeting on Sunday afternoon, at the Young Men's Christian Association Rooms. A very inter- esting address was give.n by Mr. H. Taylor (Police Court Missionary),the subject being "Wild Flowers and their Teaching." The P.S .A. quatette party contributed two quartettes, Miss Kent and Mr. A. Baker took the solo parts. Mr. He dgecock and Mr. Gregson also took part in the meeting. Arrange- ments were made to hold a harvest festival. ST. PETER'S CHURCH COMMITTEE MEETING. A meeting of the committee of collectors in con- nection with the church boundiry wall was held in the vestry on Monday evening. The collectors re- ported that the amount standing to their Credit was £36. A discussion took place as to the advisability of immediately proceeding with the building of the wall, especially the part fronting Birchall-street. The churchwardens stated that it would cast nearly £2OO to build the wall round the church, but that the part fronting Birchall-street might be built for about £6O. It was considered that funds might be obtained to build the front portion,but that it would be impossible to raise the larger sum To enable the committee to do even this they required about £25 to £3O more. The collectors have up to the present time confined themselves to their own parish. The parish is a very poor one, and requires outside assistance. A sub-committee was appointed to further consider the matter and retort to a meeting shortly to be called. WESLEYAN QUARTERLY MEETING. The above meeting was held on Monday in the Bold-street schoolroom. Alter tea had been par- taken of the meeting was presided over by the Rev. 3. Kirtlan. who was supported by the circuit minis- ters and stewards together with a large numbar of representatives. The total number of members reported was 1,052, being an increase of four in the quarter. 17 persons had ceased to be members, 19 new members had been received and 36 remained on trial. A small committee of advice was appointed to take into consideration the working of the Parish Councils Act; particularly in those places where the Wesleyan Ch urch is represented. Attention was drawn to the new legislation which provides that all Sunday schools shall be repre- sented in the quarterly meeting. It was announced that the Liverpool Synod will hold its sittings next May, in Warrington. The forthcoming arrangement for the foreign missionary meetings were announced and the day of initiation on behalf of foreign mis- sions, appointed by the conference, is to be observed throughout the. circuit on the first Friday in October.

PENKETII.

PROJECTED Co-OPERATIVE SOCIETT.—On Monday night a meeting was held in the gay Star Cottage to consider the advisability of floating a Co-operative Society for the production of certain branches of the leather trade. The scheme originated in Mr. C. E. Parker, PIRO had for some time been desirous of seeing some such business started, and which he thinks will greatly benefit the village in general. Mr. Parker was in the chair and there was a crowded audience. In a long speech he described the benefits to be derived from the business, ' which had already obtained a large output in its initia- tory stage at the Tanning Company's premises. The meeting was very enthusiastic and took up a large num- ber of shares. Several questions were put to the chair- man, as to whether this project was likely to meet the fate ofithe Park scheme. Mr. Parker replied that he thought not. Messrs. Watson, Routledge, Woods, Lewis, Houghton, Moores, Norman and others took InTt in the discussion of the meeting. It was eventually agreed that the company should be started with 500 pound shares, the premises at present to be at the Tanning Company's Works, and the new Company purchase the plant there situated. Shares to the amount of over £4OO were taken up, the highest number for one person being 100. The new company have every expectation of working up an extensive business. No mention was made of the prob- able dividend, but as far as can be ascertained it will be fairly large.

DARING ROBBERY OF DIAMONDS.

£5,000 WORTH STOLEN. Shortly before four o'clock on Thursday afternoon week daring robbery of diamonds was committed at No. 70, Hatton Garden, London. Rooms were taken some time back b 7 three men of foreign appearance,who circulated the statement that they were purchasing diamonds for Continental markets. About three o'clock on Thursday one of the men instructed an outside broker to procure a parcel of" three-grain" stones, which is a technical expression applied to a quantity of diamonds of that par- ticular size. Mr. Spiers, a Dutch merchant, hearing of the demand and having a stock of the gems, called at No. 70 with a packet-book containing diamonds and other precious stones, amounting in value to £5,000. This would he about four o'clock. At half-past five o'clock Mr. Spiers was observed to stagger across the road, with his faee cvered with blood. One of his eyes was seriously injured. His coat was torn open and his hat had gone. When sufficiently recovered Mr. Spiers made a statement to the eff-ct that his pocket-book and contents had been stolen. Immediately upon entering the house a cloth, saturated with chloroform,was tightly bound across his mouth. In the violent struggle that ensued he received the injuries to his face. It is be- lieved, from the careful planning and carrying out of the robbery, that the perpetrators are associated with if not the actual gang of theivea who some four years ago hired appartments in Hatton Garden and carefully carried out the theft of the Cape mail bag, full of valu- ables, the postman on that occasion being chloroformed after a violent struggle, the thieves making good their escape. The Press Association, telegraphing yesterday after noon, states that no arrests have yet been made in con- nection with the flatten Garden diamond robbery. Later inquiry shows that the person by whom the daring rob- bery was planned has frequented the Diamond Club, Hatton Garden. for three months. Though not a mem- ber he visited the premises frequently, apparently for business purposes. Mr. Spiers,the victim of the robbery travels from Amsterdam weekly. It transpires that had befallen into the cleverly laid trap on Wednesday instead of on Thursday his loss would have been much greater. Mr. Spiers used to frequent the club to write letters, but was never known to avail himself of the greater security for transferring the precious stones which the institution affords. The appearance .of the first floor room at 70. Hatton Garden is instructive of the mode adopted by the thieves. Directly opposite the desk at which the principal and his unsuspecting victim were seated is a dead screen,in which are two punctures through which the confederates probably watched for the signal from their chief. On taking possession of this room the mysterious tenant caused the name of F. G. Morris to be painted on the fanlight, and on Monday this name was Placed on the ordinary plate at the main entrance. The front windows of the rooms had been " frosted " to screen the occupants from observation from the windows of the premises opposite. Mr. Cullmann, pearl dealer, occupies a room immediately bellind this one,but he and his wife were absent from the building at the time. Mrs. Cullmann states that even had they been there and heard noises they would probably have ascribed it to the moving of furniture brought in by the new tenant. The daring and even reckless character of the robbery is shown by the fact that it was perpetrated in a building which is a perfect network of offices belonging to differ- ' ent firms, and within a few feet of each other. So far the detectives engaged in the case are without any clue likely to lead to the detection and arrest of the thieves.

SHOCKING NEGLECT OF CHILDREN AT

WARRINGTON.

On Friday, at the Warrington Borough Court, John and Alice Clare, Stamford-yard, and Wm. and Annie Taylor, Warburton-street, were charged at the instance of Inspector McAteer, N.S.P.C.C., with unlawfully and wilfully neglecting their children. Mr. J. D. Buckton, solicitor, prosecuted on behalf of the Society. In the case of the two first named defendants, Mr. Buckton stated that the previous week the case was adjourned in order for the male defendant to appear. The Inspector had ascertained that he was receiving medical treatment, and he was unable therefore to appear. The two children were still being neglected. The magistrates adjourned the case for 14 days and advispd the female defendant to take the children to the Workhouse.—ln the case of Taylor, Mr. Buckton stated that they had four children, two boys and two girls. The house when visited was in a filthy dirty state, and the children were no better. The bedding was filthy and rotten with the filth. In the house there was no proper food. The children wore very little clothing and were greatly neglected. The husnand was spoken of as a decent man and there was no doubt he would not allow it, if he could control his wife. It was the wife's crav- ing for drink that was the cause of the neglect, and her- husband had no control over her. He earned 355. per week and gave his wife 255. and this sum it appeared was alnwst entirely spent in drink. Inspector MCATEER deposed that on the 4th of the present month, in company with P.C. Dean, he visited the defendant's house. He found the female defendant and her four children there. The eldest child, Mary Ann, was very dirty, and was wearing one or two old garments. Her hair was matted together and was covered with vermin. The boy James was also very dirty and appeared as if he had not been washed for a long time. The girl, Annie, was wearing two old frocks and her hair was completely alive with vermin. Th baby, nine months old, witness thought had not been washed for two days. He examined the premises and found no food of any description. The kitchen was very dirty and had not been washed fOr a long time. He went upstairs, and in the room where three of the children slept the bed consisted of a dirty, rotten sack, saturated with urine. In the back room where the de- fendants slept with the youngest child, there was an old bag lying on the floor, a dirty pillow, but no bed. There was no furniture whatever in the two rooms. He asked the female defendant how she accounted for it, but she refused to tell him anything. In a cupboard, witness found a can containing beer, which he considered had been bought that morning. He called at the house two days afterwards, and found the house and children in a similar state. He saw the husband, who was a painter, the previous Wednesday, and he told witness that he earned 355. a week, and he allowed his wife 255. He also said that he coull do nothing to stop it, as he had no control over his wife. His foreman gave him a very good character, and witness believed he was a sober man. P.C. DEAN gave corroborative evidence, and said that the children's heads were alive. On being shown to the female defendant she said she could not see them. Dur- ing the time they were there a woman who kept an off licensed house asked them to look over it this time as she was a very good customer of hers. The male defendant said the children had always had plenty of food, if they had been neglected other- wise. He admitted his wife had been very careless,but she had got drinking with neighbours. He asked the magistrates not to send her to gaol. The magistrates dismissed the case against Mr. Taylor but fined his wife 20s. and costs or one month's imprison ment with hard labour. The money was paid. As they were leaving the Court, the Chairman told them th at he hoped the case would be a warning to them, at which Mrs. Taylor replied that it would be a warnine,and said she would take care that it never occurred again.
